L48: ## 1 Executive Summary
L49: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L50: Etrasimod is an orally bioavailable sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator that binds with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5. S1P receptor modulation partially and reversibly sequesters specific lymphocytes in lymph nodes, thus reducing activated lymphocytes in the tissue. The proposed indication is for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
L51: S1P signaling is involved in multiple immune functions. The mechanism by which etrasimod exerts therapeutic effects involves lymphocyte retention in lymphoid tissues and the reduction of lymphocyte migration to sites of inflammation including the intestine. As UC is an immune-mediated inflammatory disease, the retention of lymphocytes in the lymphoid tissue has the potential to prevent recruitment of inflammatory cells, local release of proinflammatory cytokines, and ongoing damage to the colonic mucosa. Reduction of this inflammatory response may allow a decrease in disease activity and subsequent healing of the mucosa.
L52: Etrasimod is a new molecular entity (NME) for UC. Other S1P modulators have been approved for UC (e.g., Zeposia [ozanimod]), multiple sclerosis, psoriasis, and graft-versus-host disease. The proposed dosing for etrasimod in UC is 2 mg orally once daily.
L53: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L54: The multidisciplinary review team recommends approval of this original NDA for Velsipity (etrasimod) for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adults.
L55: The Applicant submitted the results of two adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials (APD334-301 and APD334-302), which formed the basis of the substantial evidence of effectiveness. A clinically meaningful and statistically significant benefit was demonstrated on the coprimary endpoints for APD334-301 and primary endpoint for APD334-302. Multiple secondary endpoints supported the primary efficacy results in each trial. Thus, the Applicant has met the evidentiary standard for efficacy to support approval of etrasimod for the proposed indication.
L56: The safety profile was acceptable and generally consistent with known adverse reactions for drugs in the same class. Identified risks will be mitigated through labeling.
L57: The overall benefit-risk assessment supports approval as described in the Benefit-Risk Assessment below.
L58: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L59: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L60: Moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) is a serious chronic disease associated with morbidity and mortality when inadequately treated. Multiple therapies are approved for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC; however, there remains a need for novel therapies as not all patients will respond or have continued response to any given treatment.
L61: Etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in inducing clinical remission after 12 weeks of treatment with 2 mg daily, and in maintaining clinical remission when continued over 40 weeks of treatment. Efficacy was demonstrated in two adequate and well-controlled investigations:
L62: - APD334-301: A 52-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled “treat-through” trial
- APD334-302: A 12-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled induction trial
L63: In the 52-week, “treat-through” trial, etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in achieving the coprimary endpoints of clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52, compared to placebo. In the 12-week induction trial, etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in achieving the primary endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12, compared to placebo. In both trials, efficacy was demonstrated on multiple clinically relevant secondary endpoints at Week 12 (APD334-301 and APD334-302) and Week 52 (APD334-301), compared to placebo. Overall, the results provided substantial evidence of effectiveness for etrasimod in the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adults. Therefore, the Applicant has met the evidentiary standard for efficacy to support approval of etrasimod for the proposed indication.
L64: The safety profile of etrasimod 2 mg once daily for up to 52 weeks was acceptable and supports approval of the dosing regimen. The safety database included data from the 52-week phase 3 trial APD334-301, the 12-week phase 3 trial APD334-302, and 12-week phase 2 trial APD334-003. The size of the safety database was adequate to support approval of the proposed dosing regimen. However, the size of the controlled 52-week trial does not allow for a definitive characterization of the potential for rare, serious safety concerns (e.g., mortality, malignancy, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy [PML], cryptococcal meningitis) that have been observed in S1P receptor modulators, including ozanimod (ZEPOSIA) that is approved for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. There are several known potential risks related to the S1P receptor modulator class which were observed in the etrasimod development program (i.e., infections, bradyarrhythmia and atrioventricular conduction delays, liver injury, macular edema, increased blood pressure, and respiratory effects), which were consistent with other S1P receptor modulators. The most common adverse reactions observed in UC subjects treated with etrasimod during the 52-week trial
L65: APD334-301 were headache (9%), elevated liver tests (6%), and dizziness (5%). Additionally, a greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an increase of at least 40 \mum in macular thickness compared to placebo (16/95, 17% vs 2/26, 8%), which is concerning for macular edema. The interpretation of this finding is challenging because a limited number of subjects had optical coherence tomography (OCT) performed at baseline and throughout the trial. The most common adverse reactions during the 12-week trials (APD334-302 and APD334-003) were generally similar to the 52-week trial. The labeling is considered adequate to communicate these risks and provide appropriate risk mitigation.
L66: Based on the above considerations, the overall benefit-risk assessment is favorable to support approval of etrasimod for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
L67: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Ulcerative colitis (UC), an inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), is a chronic, relapsing disease of the colonic mucosa. The peak age of occurrence is between the ages of 30 and 40. UC may affect as many as 907,000 Americans; the annual incidence rates of UC in the United States range from 1.55 to 15.0 cases per 100,000 person-years, and the prevalence ranges from 117 to 238 cases per 100,000 persons.
Patients with UC most commonly present with bloody diarrhea, rectal bleeding, tenesmus, urgency, abdominal pain, and passage of mucus in the stool. Disease of moderate to severe activity may be associated with systemic symptoms, including fatigue, fever, anorexia, nausea, weight loss, and dehydration. Patients may also experience symptoms secondary to anemia and hypoalbuminemia, including dyspnea and peripheral edema.
UC is associated with many extraintestinal manifestations, which have been reported to affect a wide variety of organ systems, most commonly joints, skin, eyes, kidneys, and hepatobiliary tract.
UC is a chronic, relapsing inflammatory disorder of the colonic mucosa.
Patients with UC commonly present with bloody diarrhea, rectal bleeding, tenesmus, urgency, abdominal pain, and passage of mucus in the stool. Left untreated, patients may experience significant morbidity and/or mortality.
L68: ## NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L69: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	The overall goal in the treatment and management of UC is to reduce signs and symptoms of active disease, decrease the underlying mucosal inflammation, and prevent short-term (e.g., severe bleeding, bowel perforation) and long-term complications (e.g., colon cancer). The choice of therapy is guided by the disease severity, extent of disease, and presence of other manifestations (i.e., extra-intestinal complications, malabsorption).
Therapeutic options for treatment include 5-ASA products (e.g., mesalamine), corticosteroids, antibiotics, immunomodulators (e.g., AZA, 6-MP, MTX), biologic therapies (e.g., TNF\alpha blockers, anti-integrin receptor blockers), S1P receptor modulators, and JAK inhibitors. Corticosteroids are not recommended for long-term use given the toxicities associated with chronic steroid use. While these medications are widely used in clinical practice, not all are FDA approved for the treatment of UC. There remains a need for novel therapies as not all patients will respond or have continued response to any given treatment.
Etrasimod is a S1P receptor modulator with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5, proposed for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Another S1P receptor modulator (that binds with high affinity to S1P receptors 1 and 5), ozanimod (ZEPOSIA), is approved for the same indication.
Additional therapies are needed to treat moderately to severely active UC, as many patients do not respond or lose response over time to the currently available therapies. Etrasimod represents another oral, daily treatment option for patients.

Benefit	Efficacy of etrasimod was demonstrated in one 52-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled “treat-through” trial (APD334-301) and one 12-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled induction trial (APD334-302).
In both trials, subjects with moderately to severely active UC were enrolled. A total of 408 adult subjects with a baseline modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 5 to 9, including an endoscopic subscore (ES) of at least 2, were enrolled in APD334-301, of which 121 had prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure. A total of 333 adult subjects with the same definition of moderately to severely active UC were enrolled in APD334-302, of which 112 had prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure.
The two trials, APD334-301 and APD334-302, provided evidence for the effectiveness of etrasimod compared to placebo for achieving clinical remission in subjects with moderately to severely active UC.
Subgroup analyses of subjects with prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure support that these subjects, who are typically more refractory to treatment than biologic-naïve subjects, also derived benefit from
L70: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
In APD334-301, efficacy was demonstrated on the coprimary endpoints of clinical remission (defined as a mMS score of 0 to 2 with a stool frequency subscore [SFS] of 0 or 1, rectal bleeding subscore [RBS] of 0, and ES of 0 or 1 [excluding friability]) at Week 12 and Week 52. The treatment difference in the proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission between etrasimod and placebo was 20.1% (p < 0.0001) and 25.7% (p < 0.0001) for Week 12 and Week 52, respectively.The coprimary endpoint results were further investigated in subgroup analyses for subjects with prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure. Efficacy results were consistent across both subgroups. The treatment difference for clinical remission between etrasimod and placebo was 15.1% and 16.4% for Week 12 and Week 52, respectively, in the prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure subgroup.
The majority of the secondary endpoints were statistically significant, including endoscopic improvement (Week 12 and 52), histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement (Week 12 and 52), corticosteroid-free clinical remission (Week 52), and maintenance of clinical remission (Week 52).

In APD334-302, efficacy was demonstrated on the primary endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12. The treatment difference in the proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission between etrasimod and placebo was 11.2% (p = 0.010). Although the treatment difference observed for APD334-302 was smaller than that observed for APD334-301, baseline demographic and disease characteristics were similar between APD334-301 and APD334-302. The difference observed was attributed to the intrinsic variability of the placebo
treatment with etrasimod.

Risk and Risk Management
The safety of etrasimod was evaluated in two adequate and well-controlled trials, APD334-301 (52-week duration) and APD334-302 (12-week duration). Additionally, data from APD334-003, a phase 2, 12-week, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled induction trial was pooled with data from APD334-302 because the two trial designs and populations were sufficiently similar, to better assess the adverse event profile for the 12-week trials.
The safety profile of etrasimod for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC appears to be consistent with the labeled safety profile of other S1P receptor modulators, including ozanimod, which is approved for the same indication.
The label contains warnings and precautions for the following, which are consistent with other S1P receptor modulators:Infections
Bradyarrhythmia and Atrioventricular Conduction Delays
Liver Injury
Macular Edema
Increased Blood Pressure

The demonstrated and anticipated risks will be managed through labeling, including the following:Infections – Obtain a complete blood count (CBC) before initiation of treatment and monitor for infection during treatment and for (b)(4) weeks after discontinuation. Consider interruption of treatment with etrasimod if a serious infection develops.
Bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction delays – Obtain an electrocardiogram (ECG) to assess for preexisting cardiac conduction abnormalities before starting

L83: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L84: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L85: Ulcerative colitis (UC) is a chronic gastrointestinal inflammatory disorder that involves the colonic surface mucosa, including the infiltration of neutrophils and other inflammatory cells into the epithelium and submucosa of the colon. The etiology of UC is multifactorial, but likely includes a dysregulated mucosal immune response against commensal non-pathogenic bacteria of the colon, resulting in bowel inflammation. Onset of disease most commonly occurs between 15 and 40 years of age. Prevalence estimates per 100,000 persons in North America range from 139.8 (Quebec, Canada) to 286.3 (Minnesota, USA), while in Europe they range from 2.42 (Romania, 2003) to 505 (Norway, 1991-94), including 412 in Germany (2010), 340 in Hungary (2013), and 90.8 in the UK (1989). Globally, the prevalence of UC continues to rise, particularly in North America and Europe, and is expected to increase in newly industrialized countries in Africa, Asia, and South America (Ng et al. 2017).
L86: The clinical course of UC is characterized by a lifelong course of remissions and exacerbations. Patients with UC suffer from recurrent episodes of diarrhea, rectal bleeding, weight loss, abdominal pain, fever, and are at an increased risk of perforated bowel, toxic megacolon, and colorectal cancer. The estimated risk of colorectal cancer is approximately 2% after 10 years, 5% to 10% after 20 years, and 12% to 30% after 30 to 35 years of UC (Bernstein et al. 2001; Eaden, Abrams, and Mayberry 2001; Feuerstein and Cheifetz 2014).
L87: Patients have a 10% cumulative risk of colectomy 5 years after diagnosis, and 15% at 10 years after diagnosis (Fumery et al. 2018). However, with colectomy, there is a 50% risk of continued inflammation in the residual intestinal pouch (pouchitis); after 10 years, approximately 12% of patients experience pouch failure and require conversion to a permanent ileostomy (Zezos and Saibil 2015; Mark-Christensen et al. 2018). Surgical complications of proctocolectomy with ileostomy include stenosis, prolapse, and other abdominal/pelvic sequelae including small bowel obstruction, fistula, infection, persistent pain, unhealed perineal wound, sexual and bladder dysfunction, and infertility.
L88: Patients with UC may also experience extra-intestinal manifestations including primary sclerosing cholangitis or eye, joint, or skin manifestations. Improved intestinal disease activity in UC is associated with an improvement in extra-intestinal manifestations.
L89: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L90: The goals of UC treatment include decreasing mucosal inflammation, reducing signs and symptoms, reducing long-term corticosteroid use, reducing colon cancer risk, and improving patient quality of life.
L91: Treatment of UC is challenging due to its multifactorial nature and incompletely understood pathogenesis. UC is also a chronic disease, and in general, most drugs that are initiated for induction of remission are continued as maintenance therapy, if they are effective (Feuerstein et al. 2020).
L92: Therapeutic options for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC include a variety of pharmacologic therapies such as tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-\alpha antagonists (infliximab, adalimumab, golimumab), anti-integrin agents (vedolizumab), Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors (tofacitinib, upadacitinib), interleukin (IL) 12/23 antagonist (ustekinumab), sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator (ozanimod), and other immunomodulators (thiopurines, methotrexate). However, a proportion of patients who have initially responded to these therapies may subsequently lose response and experience recurrence of UC symptoms. Recent systematic reviews and meta-analyses have reported that the estimated incidence rates of loss of response (LOR) to vedolizumab was 39.8 (per 100 person-years of follow-up) in patients with UC (Peyrin-Biroulet et al. 2019) and the loss of response to anti-TNF agents was approximately 25% in one year and in 33% in 3 years in patients with IBD (Guberna et al. 2021).
L93: Although multiple approved therapies are available, there remains a need for novel therapies for patients who have had an inadequate response, intolerance, or loss of response to available therapies and would benefit from the availability of additional therapeutic options.
L94: The currently approved systemic therapies for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC are summarized in Table 1.

L110: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L111: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L112: At the time of this review, etrasimod has not been authorized for marketing in any country outside of the U.S.
L113: On October 14, 2023, Pfizer, Inc. submitted a new drug application (NDA) for new molecular entity (NME), etrasimod. Etrasimod (also referred to as APD334, APD334 L-arginine or AR401959) is a S1P receptor modulator. The proposed indication for etrasimod is the treatment moderately to severely active UC in adults at a recommended dose of 2 mg orally once daily.

A "treat-through" design appeared reasonable

The proposal to modify visit windows due to the COVID-19 pandemic appeared reasonable.

L124: ## Financial Disclosure
L125: Etrasimod was originally developed by Arena Pharmaceuticals Inc. (Arena), which was acquired by Pfizer Inc. (Pfizer) on March 11, 2022. The two covered clinical studies (APD334-301 and APD334-302) were conducted, completed, and achieved database lock before the completion of this acquisition. Per guidance received from FDA in Pre-NDA meeting minutes (Reference ID 4992019) dated May 31, 2022, since the acquisition occurred within 1 year of conclusion of the studies, both Arena and Pfizer are considered sponsors for the covered clinical studies. As such, the financial disclosures were provided pertaining to both entities (collectively “Applicant”).
L126: Financial disclosure information was self-disclosed by investigators. All investigators were assessed for equity interest, significant payments of other sorts, other compensation by the Sponsor, and propriety interest. Self-disclosures pertaining to Arena have been collected from 97% of investigators. Per FDA’s request communicated in the same Pre-NDA meeting minutes, the Applicant has made efforts to collect updated financial disclosure information from investigators identifying both Arena and Pfizer as sponsors. As of the disclosure cutoff date of September 6, 2022, for this NDA, 33% of investigators have provided the requested updates; due diligence to obtain these updates will continue for 1 year after study completion for each covered clinical study.
L127: Seven investigators had reportable financial interests of any significant payments from the Applicant for the covered study, APD334-301, such as a grant to fund ongoing research, compensation in the form of equipment, retainer for ongoing consultation, or honoraria. The seven investigators represent 1% (7/793) of the investigators for APD334-301. Given the total number of investigators, the potential impact of this financial interest was not expected to influence the trial outcome.
L128: Five investigators had reportable financial interests of any significant payments from the Applicant for the covered study, APD334-302, such as a grant to fund ongoing research, compensation in the form of equipment, retainer for ongoing consultation, or honoraria. The five investigators represent 0.5% (5/933) of the investigators for APD334-302. Given the total number of investigators, the potential impact of this financial interest was not expected to influence the trial outcome.

L136: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L137: ## 4.2.1. Drug Substance
L138: Etrasimod arginine is the arginine salt of the active moiety etrasimod and is a synthetic small molecule sphingosine 1-phosphate receptor modulator. Etrasimod has not been previously approved or marketed in the United States and therefore, it is classified as a New Molecular Entity (NME).
L139: Etrasimod arginine is a white, off-white, to light brown crystalline solid. It has a melting point of 203.5C -208.1C, a pKa of 4.5, and a log P of 6.8 – 9.0. It is non-hygrosopic with a solubility of 1.38mg/mL in water at pH of 8.9. To date, one thermodynamically stable crystalline form has been identified for etrasimod arginine. The drug substance as free acid has one chiral center, and the R-configuration has been confirmed by single crystal X-ray technique.
L140: Etrasimod arginine is the L-arginine salt of (R)-2-(7-(4-cyclopentyl-3-(trifluoromethyl)benzyloxy)-1,2,3,4-tetrahydrocyclopenta[b]indol-3-yl) acetic acid. It has a molecular formula of C32H40F3N5O5 (C26H26F3NO3 as free acid), a molecular weight of 631.69 g/mol (457.48 g/mol as free acid), and the chemical structure as shown in Figure 1.
L141: **Figure 1. Etrasimod Arginine Structural Formula**
L142: Source: General Information, 2.3.S Drug Substance, Page 2 of 3.
L143: The drug substance, etrasimod arginine, for this application is manufactured in accordance with current Good Manufacturing Practice (cGMP) requirements by AMPAC™ Fine Chemicals, LLC., Rancho Cordova, CA. It is tested and released against a specification that assures identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug substance at release and throughout its proposed re-test period of (b)(4) months. The proposed re-test date of (b)(4) months is supported by the stability data provided in the application and is granted. Based on the results from stability testing of this drug substance, it should be stored at (b)(4).
L144: ## 4.2.2. Drug Product
L145: The drug product, Velsipity (etrasimod) Tablets, 2 mg is indicated for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis in adults. The recommended dosage is one tablet taken orally once daily. The tablet should be swallowed as a whole with or without food.
L146: Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are green, round, film-coated immediate release tablets debossed with “ETR” on one side and “2” on the other side. Each tablet contains 2.762 mg of etrasimod arginine equivalent to 2 mg of etrasimod as the active ingredient and magnesium stearate, mannitol, microcrystalline cellulose, and sodium starch glycolate, with a film coating containing FD&amp;C blue #1/brilliant blue FCF aluminum lake, FD&amp;C blue #2/indigo carmine aluminum lake, FD&amp;C yellow #5/tartrazine aluminum lake, macrogol 4000 JP/PEG 3350, polyvinyl alcohol (partially hydrolyzed), talc, and titanium dioxide as inactive ingredients.
L147: Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are supplied as 30 tablets in a child resistant 100 mL high density polyethylene (HDPE) white, round bottle with 4 g silica gel as desiccant integrated directly into the 45 mm white polypropylene (PP) closure.
L148: Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are manufactured in accordance with cGMP requirements by Penn Pharmaceutical Services Limited, United Kingdom and it is tested and released against a specification that assures the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug product at release and throughout its proposed expiration dating period of 24 months. The proposed
L149: expiration dating period of 24 months is supported by the stability data provided in the application and is granted. This drug product should be stored at 20°C to 25°C (68°F to 77°F); excursions permitted between 15°C to 30°C (59°F to 86°F) [see USP Controlled Room Temperature].

L157: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L158: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L159: From a nonclinical perspective, no approvability issues have been identified for etrasimod at the proposed clinical dose of 2 mg/day.
L160: Nonclinical studies conducted with etrasimod include pharmacology studies, safety pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetics, repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to six months duration in rats and nine months duration in dogs, a standard genotoxicity battery, two-year carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats, a standard battery of developmental and reproductive toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, and an in vitro phototoxicity study.
L161: Etrasimod is a sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1, 4, and 5 (S1P_{1,4,5}) agonist with no significant pharmacological activity at S1P_2 or S1P_3 receptors. Etrasimod agonism of S1P_1 reduces lymphocyte egress from lymph nodes, resulting in decreased circulating lymphocytes.
L162: In a standard battery of nonclinical safety pharmacology studies, etrasimod had no significant effects on hERG channel current in vitro, cardiovascular function in dogs, CNS parameters in rats, or respiratory function in rats. In an in vitro assay using human atrial cardiomyocytes, etrasimod activated IK_{ACh} channels at concentrations less than the predicted steady state C_{max} and C_{trough} values at the clinical etrasimod dose of 2 mg/day; this finding may provide an underlying mechanism for the transient etrasimod-induced decrease in heart rate observed in clinical trials.
L163: In repeat-dose toxicity studies, mortalities, decreased body weight gain, and histopathological findings in the liver and thymus were observed in rats at exposures &gt; 1000-fold the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 2 mg/day (based on AUC comparison). No adverse effects were observed in rats or dogs at exposures up to 738- and 446-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD (based on AUC comparison). Dose-dependent decreases in circulating lymphocytes were observed in both rats and dogs and correlated with lymphoid depletion in the lymph nodes and spleen of rats only; these findings were attributed to the pharmacological activity of etrasimod and not considered adverse.
L164: Etrasimod did not demonstrate genotoxic potential in a standard battery of in vitro and in vivo studies. Process-related impurity [REDACTED](b) (4) was considered positive for mutagenicity in the bacterial reverse mutation assay and is controlled in the [REDACTED](b) (4) manufacturing product at &lt; [REDACTED](b) (4) µg/day in accordance with ICH M7(R1), option 2.
L165: In a two-year carcinogenicity study in mice, a statistically significant positive dose-response in the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined
L166: ## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L167: hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed at exposures \ge 46-fold the exposure at the MRHD (based on AUC comparison). In a two-year carcinogenicity study in rats, no evidence of carcinogenic potential was observed at exposures up to 88- and 198-fold the exposure at the MRHD in males and females, respectively (based on AUC comparison). The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with these conclusions. Notably, these findings are consistent with those observed in studies with other approved S1P receptor modulators.
L168: No adverse effects of etrasimod were observed on male or female fertility parameters at doses up to 42 and 121 times, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD (based on AUC comparison). In an embryo-fetal development study in rats, no maternal toxicity was observed at up to 23-fold the exposure at the MRHD, but a developmental NOAEL could not be determined based on the observed dose-dependent increase in the incidence of post-implantation loss (primarily early resorptions), decreased number and proportion of viable fetuses, and dose-dependent incidence of fetal malformations (aortic vessel abnormalities and septal defects) at exposures \ge 6 times the exposure at the MRHD. In an embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, no maternal toxicity was observed at up to 12 times the exposure at MRHD. Based on the findings of increased post-implantation loss, decreased litter proportions of viable fetuses, and increased fetal malformations (aortic arch defects and fused sternebrae) at exposures 7 and 11 times the exposure at the MRHD, the NOAEL for developmental toxicity in rabbits was considered the low dose of 2 mg/kg/day (approximately the same exposure as MRHD). In a pre- and post-natal development study in rats, maternal toxicity including the premature termination of one female during delivery, increased post-implantation loss, increased number of females with stillborn pups, increased number of stillborn pups per litter, decreased viability index, and decreased lactation index was observed at exposures 10 and 24 times the exposure at the MRHD; thus, the NOAEL for maternal toxicity was considered the low dose of 0.4 mg/kg/day (approximately 2 times the exposure at the MRHD). Based on findings of increased perinatal mortality, adverse clinical signs and findings of decreased body weight/body weight gain during the preweaning period, as well as reduced number of implantations and increased rate of pre-implantation loss correlated with a decrease in the number of live embryos per litter, a developmental NOAEL could not be determined (with effects occurring at exposures less than that predicted at the MRHD).
L169: No novel excipients are used in the formulation of the etrasimod drug product, and all excipients are used at acceptable levels that are consistent with those used in other FDA approved, orally administered products. No leachables are expected given the solid tablet formulation. Elemental impurities in the etrasimod drug substance meet the allowable limits established by ICH Q3D, option 1, for oral administration. All impurities, including those with genotoxic potential, are controlled in accordance with ICH Q3A and/or ICH M7(R1) as appropriate. Thus, there are no safety concerns regarding excipients, impurities, or extractables and leachables from the drug product or container closure system.
L170: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L171: None.
L172: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L173: Etrasimod is a sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1, 4, and 5 (S1P_{1,4,5}) agonist with no significant pharmacological activity at S1P_2 or S1P_3 receptors. At clinically relevant concentrations, etrasimod had no significant binding or activity when tested against panels of other receptors, ion channels, transporters, and enzymes. Etrasimod agonism of S1P_1 reduces lymphocyte egress from lymph nodes, resulting in decreased circulating lymphocytes. In nonclinical species, etrasimod produced a dose-dependent decrease in blood lymphocytes, with EC_{50} values of 25 ng/mL in rats and dogs and 45 ng/mL in mice and monkeys (approximately 4.5 and 2.5 times lower, respectively, than the steady state C_{max} at the clinical etrasimod dose of 2 mg/day; 113 ng/mL).
L174: A standard battery of nonclinical safety pharmacology studies did not demonstrate significant safety concerns regarding acute etrasimod administration. Etrasimod inhibited hERG signaling in vitro, with a maximal inhibitory effect of 59% at the highest tested dose of 3 \muM; a specific IC_{50} value could not be determined based on the study design but is likely between 1000-3000 nM (460-1370 ng/mL; 4-12 times the steady state C_{max} at the MRHD). In an in vitro assay using human atrial cardiomyocytes, etrasimod activated IK_{ACh} channels (EC_{50}: 29.9 nM or 18.9 ng/mL; 6 times lower than the steady state C_{max} at the MRHD). This finding may suggest an underlying mechanism for the transient drug-induced decrease in heart rate observed in clinical trials with etrasimod in various indications. A single oral dose of etrasimod did not produce acute CNS or clinically relevant respiratory effects in rats (NOELs: 350 mg/kg/day) or cardiovascular effects in dogs (NOEL: 350 mg/kg/day).

Distribution	Etrasimod is highly bound (> 95%) to plasma proteins in multiple species7

Metabolism	
In vitro
The disappearance half-life of etrasimod was >60 minutes in liver microsomes from mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human, and approximately 30 minutes in microsomes from rabbit8
Metabolites identified using human liver microsomes included M1 (glucuronide conjugate), M2 and M3 (mono-hydroxyl metabolites), as well as M4 and M5 (glucuronide conjugates of M2 and M3)9
Metabolites identified using hepatocytes from various species were 29 in total, with the most abundant including M1, M3, and M6, as well as hydroxyl glucuronides M14, M15, and M1710
M3 and M6 were both observed in hepatocytes from rat, dog, and monkey10
No unique human metabolites identified in vitro9,10
Glucuronidation of etrasimod is primarily catalyzed by UGT1A7 in liver microsomes from humans as well as mouse, rat, dog, and monkey11
In vivo
Primary human metabolites M3 and M6 were detected in the plasma and feces of rats3 and feces of dogs5
Metabolism of etrasimod in dogs involved oxidation, dehydrogenation, and conjugation via sulfate or glucuronidation5

Excretion	
Rats
Elimination primarily via the bile/feces (> 80% within 48 hours, > 95% within 168 hours post-dose)3
Dogs
Elimination primarily via the feces, with 68% recovered by 96 hours post-dose and > 81% through 168 hours post-dose5

L186: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L187: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L188: Repeat-dose toxicity studies of up to six months duration in rats and nine months duration in dogs were conducted to support the chronic use of etrasimod in humans. Mortalities, decreased body weight gain, and histopathological findings in the liver and thymus were observed in rats at exposures &gt; 1000-fold the exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC comparison. No adverse effects were observed in rats or dogs at exposures up to 738- and 446-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD.
L189: ## Study TX14009: APD334 L-Arginine: A 6-Month Oral Toxicity Study in Rats with a 4-Week Recovery Period
L190: (b) (4)
L191: Conducting laboratory and location:
L192: GLP compliance:	Yes
Dose and frequency of dosing	0, 25, 75, 150, 250 mg/kg
Route of administration	Oral gavage
Formulation/vehicle	L-arginine hydrochloride in deionized water
Species/strain	Sprague Dawley rat
Number/sex/group	15-20/sex/group
Age	6 weeks
Satellite groups/ unique design	Toxicokinetic groups: 4/sex for vehicle control, 11/sex/group treatment; 1-2 animals were included as replacement animals in each group because of mortalities
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results	No
L193: ## Key Study Findings
L194: - Etrasimod-related mortalities occurred at 150 mg/kg/day (n = 1) and 250 mg/kg/day (n = 12).
L195: - Decreased body weight gain, correlating with decreased food consumption, occurred at all doses, but most severely at 250 mg/kg/day. Findings in the liver (single-cell necrosis, hypertrophy) and thymus (hypertrophy) were considered adverse at 250 mg/kg/day due to increased severity and/or correlating changes in clinical chemistry values.
- Based on the observed mortalities, decreased body weight gain, and histopathological findings in the liver and thymus at 250 mg/kg/day, the NOAEL was considered the mid dose of 150 mg/kg/day.
L196: Etrasimod-related findings are summarized in Table 5.
L197: **Table 5. Observations and Results in the 6-Month Study in Rats (TX14009)**
L198: Parameters	Major Findings (Changes from Control)
Mortality	Two males administered 250 mg/kg/day died due to liver inflammation/necrosis, while one male administered 250 mg/kg/day male died due to urogenital inflammation and calculi.
One male in the 150 mg/kg/day group, three males in the 250 mg/kg/day group, and five females in the 250 mg/kg/day group were either found dead or terminated in extremis for undetermined reasons that were considered related to etrasimod treatment.
In the 250 mg/kg/day TK satellite groups, four males and three females were found dead or terminated in extremis with the cause of death undetermined.

Clinical Signs	Thinness, hunched posture, few or absent feces, colored material around the nose and eyes, and salivation were noted in surviving animals at 250 mg/kg/day.

Body Weights	Dose-dependent decreases in body weight gain were observed. In males, body weight gain was decreased by 11.4%, 18.9%, and 27.9% at 75, 150, and 250 mg/kg/day, respectively. In females, body weight gain was decreased by 16%, 16%, and 21.4%, respectively.
Decreases in body weight gain correlated with decreased food consumption at all doses and treatment-related adverse clinical signs at 250 mg/kg/day.
Decreased body weight gain was reversible following the 4-week recovery period in both sexes.

Ophthalmoscopy	None.
L199: Parameters	Major Findings (Changes from Control)
Hematology	Dose-dependent decreases in circulating lymphocytes (up to ~78%) and total leukocytes (up to ~70%) were observed and correlated with histopathological findings of lymphoid depletion in the lymph nodes and spleen. These are expected pharmacological effects.
Increased platelets (up to 37%), prolonged APTT (up to 2.3x), and prolonged prothrombin times (up to 2.1x) were observed at \ge 150 mg/kg/day.

Clinical Chemistry	Increased total bilirubin (up to 4.7x), creatinine (up to 93%), and urea nitrogen (up to 34%) were observed at all doses.
Increased ALT (2.1x) was observed in males administered 250 mg/kg/day and correlated with histopathological findings of hepatocyte necrosis.

Urinalysis	None.

Gross Pathology	None.

Organ Weights	Relative (to body weight) liver weight increased in males at \ge 75 mg/kg/day and females at \ge 150 mg/kg/day and correlated with histopathological findings of hepatocyte hypertrophy. These findings were reversible following the recovery period.
Relative spleen weight irreversibly decreased in all treated animals and correlated with histopathological findings of decreased lymphocytes.
Relative thyroid/parathyroid weight irreversibly increased at \ge 150 mg/kg/day and correlated with histopathological findings of thyroid follicular cell hypertrophy.

Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	In the liver, minimal to mild single cell necrosis and minimal to moderate centrilobular hepatocellular hypertrophy were observed at \ge 150 mg/kg/day.
Partially reversible, minimal to mild hyperplasia (atypical, epithelial cell, medullar lymphoid) of the thymus was observed at all doses, with moderate severity in males administered 250 mg/kg/day.

These findings are expected pharmacological effects of etrasimod, and thus were not considered adverse.

Absolute and relative thymus weights were irreversibly increased in all treated groups. These findings correlated with hematologic evidence of lymphopenia, suggesting sequestration of lymphocytes in the thymus, but no histopathological changes were reported.
Absolute and relative prostate weight were irreversibly decreased in males at all doses with no histopathological correlates.

L246: ## **5.5.3. Carcinogenicity**
L247: Etrasimod was positive for carcinogenicity in mice but negative for carcinogenicity in rats.
L248: In a two-year carcinogenicity study in mice, a statistically significant positive dose-response in the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed in males and females at 6 and 20 mg/kg/day compared to controls. These doses correspond to 46- and 134-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD of 2 mg/day (based on AUC comparison). The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with these findings and considered the study to be adequately conducted.
L249: In a two-year carcinogenicity study in rats, no carcinogenic findings were observed in males or females administered up to 20 mg/kg/day for 86 to 89 weeks, after which mortalities precluded continued conduct of the study. This dose corresponds to 88- and 198-fold the exposure at the MRHD in males and females, respectively (based on AUC comparison). The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with these findings and considered the study to be adequately conducted.

L278: ## Embryo-Fetal Development
L279: ## Dose-Range Finding Studies in Rats and Rabbits
L280: Two dose-range finding studies were conducted with etrasimod to determine the doses for the definitive embryo-fetal development (EFD) study in rats. In the first study using doses of 25, 75, 150, and 250 mg/kg/day (Study TX14016), severe maternal toxicity and decreased intrauterine survival were observed such that, among all treated groups, there was only a single viable fetus from the 25 mg/kg/day group and no others. The single fetus in the 25 mg/kg/day group (121 times the clinical dose, based on HED) was observed with a meningocele.
L281: In the second study using doses of 2, 5, 10, and 20 mg/kg/day (Study TX14025), increased post-implantation loss with corresponding lower numbers/proportions of viable fetuses was observed at \ge 5 mg/kg/day. A meningocele was observed in a single fetus each in the 5 and 10 mg/kg/day groups, and spina bifida was observed in one fetus at 10 mg/kg/day. These findings occurred at &gt; 21 times the exposure at the clinical dose, based on AUC (based on the available AUC data at 4 mg/kg/day) or 24 times the clinical dose, based on HED.
L282: While these findings suggest the potential of etrasimod exposure during organogenesis to result in neural defects, the toxicological significance of these findings is unclear due to the sporadic nature of the findings, lack of clear dose effect, and overall developmental failure at higher doses. However, the presence of these findings is unsurprising, as S1P receptors play an essential role in embryogenesis. Based on the totality of findings in these studies, the doses in the definitive EFD study in rats were set to 1, 2, and 4 mg/kg/day.
L283: In the dose-range finding study in rabbits using doses of 2, 25, 75, and 150 mg/kg/day (Study TX14018), moribundity or abortion were observed in 5/6 females at 75 mg/kg/day and all
L284: females at 150 mg/kg/day (726 and 1452 times the clinical dose, respectively, based on HED). The single surviving female at 75 mg/kg/day was observed to have increased post-implantation loss. At 25 mg/kg/day (242 times the clinical dose, based on HED), increased post-implantation loss and a lower number of viable fetuses were observed. No adverse effects were observed at 2 mg/kg/day (19 times the clinical dose, based on HED, or less than the clinical dose, based on AUC). Based on these findings, the doses in the definitive EFD study in rabbits were set to 2, 10, and 20 mg/kg/day.

L315: ## 5.5.5. Other Toxicology Studies
L316: ## Phototoxicity
L317: Etrasimod did not demonstrate phototoxic potential in an in vitro neutral red uptake phototoxicity assay using BALB/c mouse fibroblasts.
L318: ## Excipients and Leachables
L319: Etrasimod is orally administered at a dose of 2 mg/day. The etrasimod drug product is a round, immediate release, film-coated tablet available in a 2 mg form only. There are no novel excipients or excipients of human or animal origin used in the manufacturing of the etrasimod
L320: drug product. Levels of excipients used in this drug product are similar to or lower than those used in other FDA approved, orally administered products. (b) (4)
L321: all ingredients used in the manufacture of the film coating are at levels below those used in other FDA approved, orally administered products.
L322: Due to the formulation of the etrasimod drug product as a solid tablet, there are no safety concerns with regard to leachables.
L323: ## Impurities Assessment
L324: Elemental impurities in etrasimod L-arginine drug substance meet the allowable limits established by ICH Q3D, option 1, for oral administration.
L325: A genotoxic/mutagenic hazard assessment was conducted on etrasimod L-arginine and its observed and potential impurities in accordance with ICH M7(R1), “Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities in Pharmaceuticals to Limit Potential Carcinogenic Risk.” The assessment identified eight potentially genotoxic compounds: (b) (4)
L326: (b) (4) was determined to be non-mutagenic in a definitive bacterial reverse mutation assay and is therefore being controlled in the etrasimod drug substance as a non-mutagenic impurity (Class 5) in accordance with ICH Q3A.
L327: (b) (4) was identified in silico as a Class 2 impurity and was positive for genotoxicity in a definitive bacterial reverse mutation assay. Therefore, (b) (4)
L328: in accordance with ICH M7(R1) option 2.
L329: (b) (4)
L330: (b) (4) are potential impurities in starting material (b) (4) and have been identified as a potential ICH M7(R1) Class 3 impurities. (b) (4) is controlled to the non-
L331: ## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L332: specified impurity limit of (b)(4) ppm in the starting material (b)(4), while (b)(4) is controlled by specification to a level of \le (b)(4) % in the starting material. The acceptable daily intakes for (b)(4) individually are limited to not more than (b)(4) \mug/day per ICH M7(R1) guidance on (b)(4) \mug/day), equivalent to a maximum limit of not more than (b)(4) ppm based on the clinical dose of etrasimod of 2 mg/day. The control strategies described are aligned with ICH M7(R1) option 2 for (b)(4) and option 3 for (b)(4).
L333: Three organic impurities have been observed in the etrasimod drug substance and/or drug product: (b)(4) is a process-related impurity (b)(4) while (b)(4) are both degradation products. Based on in silico mutagenicity assessments, (b)(4) were determined to be Class 5 impurities and are treated as non-mutagenic impurities in accordance with ICH M7(R1).
L334: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L335: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L336: Etrasimod is a sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator, which binds to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5. It is a new molecular entity with a proposed indication for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
L337: In support of this application, the safety and effectiveness of etrasimod were assessed in phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302 in subjects 16 years of age and older with moderately to severely active UC.
L338: The clinical pharmacology review evaluated the appropriateness of the general dosing instructions, dosage for specific patient populations, drug-drug interaction (DDI), as well as population PK (PopPK), and exposure-response (E-R) analyses.
L339: Overall, the proposed oral dosage at 2 mg daily (QD) for the general adult patient population is supported by a significantly higher proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Weeks 12 and 52 compared to placebo as well as an acceptable safety profile. No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild to severe renal impairment or mild to moderate hepatic impairment. However, use of etrasimod in patients with severe hepatic impairment is not recommended.
L340: Co-administration of etrasimod with the following drugs is not recommended:
L341: - Drugs that are moderate or strong inhibitors of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4.
- Drugs that are combined CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate) inducer.
- Drugs that are moderate or strong CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor in patients who are known to be CYP2C9 poor metabolizers.
L342: ## 6.1.1. Recommendations
L343: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed this NDA submission and found it acceptable for approval for the treatment of adult patients with moderately to severely active UC from a clinical pharmacology standpoint.
L344: ## 6.1.2. Key Review Issues
L345: Key review issues with specific recommendations and comments are summarized in Table 11 below.
L346: **Table 11. Review Issues and Recommendations**
L347: Review Issues	Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal evidence of effectiveness	The effectiveness of etrasimod for adult patients was supported by the efficacy results from the phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302. A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg QD achieved clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52 (the primary efficacy endpoint for induction and maintenance periods, respectively), compared to placebo.
General dosing instructions	The proposed oral dosage of 2 mg QD for adult patients with moderately to severely active UC is acceptable.
Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	Hepatic impairment
No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment. Use of etrasimod in patients with severe hepatic impairment is not recommended based on 57% higher mean etrasimod exposure in subjects with severe hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal liver function in combination of limited clinical data at doses higher than 2 mg.
Renal impairment
No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with any degree of renal impairment. The effect of hemodialysis on the PK of etrasimod has not been evaluated.
Drug Interactions
Co-administration of etrasimod with agents that are moderate or strong inhibitors of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 (e.g., fluconazole) is not recommended. Co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is an inducer of multiple etrasimod metabolizing enzymes, such as rifampin which induces CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate), is not recommended.
In patients who are known to be CYP2C9 poor metabolizers, co-administration of etrasimod with drugs that are a moderate or strong inhibitor of CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 is not recommended.
Bridge between the "to-be-marketed" and clinical trial formulations	The 2-mg tablet formulation used in two phase 3 clinical trials was different from the to-be-marketed formulation (2 mg tablets) and has been adequately bridged to the to-be-marketed formulation by an in vivo relative bioavailability study (Study APD334-114).
L348: Source: Reviewer's table
L349: Abbreviations: DDI, drug-drug interaction; PK, pharmacokinetics; UC, Ulcerative colitis
L350: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L351: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L352: ## Pharmacology
L353: Etrasimod is a S1P receptor modulator, which binds with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5. Etrasimod partially and reversibly blocks the capacity of lymphocytes to egress from lymphoid organs, reducing the number of lymphocytes in the peripheral blood, thereby lowering the number of activated lymphocytes in the tissue. However, the mechanism by which etrasimod exerts therapeutic effects in UC is not fully known.
L354: ## Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L355: Clinical pharmacokinetics of etrasimod have been characterized in healthy subjects and subjects with UC and briefly summarized below. Overall, the pharmacokinetics of etrasimod is similar in healthy subjects and subjects with UC.
L356: ## Absorption
L357: Following oral administration, the T_{max} of etrasimod is approximately 4 hours (ranging 2 – 8 hours). Following single dose administration from 0.1 mg to 5 mg, the C_{max} and AUC for etrasimod increase proportionally by 59 and 55-fold, respectively, with a 50-fold increase in dose. Following the multiple dose administration from 0.7 mg QD to 2 mg QD, the C_{max} and AUC_{tau} at steady-state increased more than dose proportionally by 3.67 and 3.63-fold, respectively, with a 2.86-fold increase in dose. Steady state was reached by 7 days following 2 mg QD dosing, with a mean C_{max} of 113 ng/mL and AUC_{tau} of 2162 h*ng/mL.
L358: ## Food effect
L359: No clinically significant differences in the C_{max} and AUC of etrasimod were observed following administration with a high-fat, high-calorie meal, compared to the fasted conditions (&lt; 2%). Food intake can result in delayed absorption (the median T_{max}) by 2 hours.
L360: ## Distribution
L361: The mean apparent volume of distribution of etrasimod (Vz/F) is 66 L. Human plasma proteins binding of etrasimod is approximately 97.9%.
L362: ## Elimination
L363: The mean plasma elimination t_{1/2} of etrasimod is approximately 30 hours. The T_{1/2} of the two most abundant metabolites, M3 and M6, ranged from 46 to 83 hours and 52 to 70 hours, respectively.
L364: ## Metabolism
L365: Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple enzymes via oxidation, dehydrogenation,  glucuronidation, sulfation and a combination of these reactions, though unchanged etrasimod  is the main circulating component in plasma as shown in Figure 2. In vitro assays indicate that  etrasimod is metabolized via CYP2C8 (38%), CYP2C9 (37%), and CYP3A4 (22%), and with minor  contribution (1%) via CYP2C19 and CYP2J2. The two most abundant circulating metabolites of  etrasimod, AR503641 (M3) and AR504344 (M6), accounted for &lt;20% of the parent AUC as well  as &lt;10% of total radioactivity in human plasma. In vitro studies indicated that M3 was formed  primarily by CYP2C9*1 and to a lesser extent by CYP2C8 and CYP2C19. M3 was further  metabolized to M6.
L366: **Figure 2. Metabolic Pathways for Etrasimod and Metabolites in Human**
L367: Source: Clinical Study Report APD334-107, Figure 5
L368: Etrasimod has one chiral center. Analysis of cross-subject pooled plasma samples obtained  from Study APD334-005 indicated that etrasimod does not undergo any in vivo  stereoconversion to its opposite enantiomer, AR401967.
L369: ## Excretion
L370: Following a single oral dose of radiolabeled etrasimod 2 mg, approximately 82% of the  administered radioactivity was recovered in feces and 4.9% in urine over 336 hours. 57.2% of  the total administered radioactivity was recovered in feces by 144 hours post-dose. In feces,  11.2% was unchanged etrasimod and 41% was metabolites. Unchanged etrasimod was not  detected in urine.
L371: *Version date: October 12, 2018 *
L372: ## PK in Subjects with Ulcerative Colitis
L373: The PK of etrasimod was characterized in subjects with UC by population PK analysis using sparse PK samples collected in phase 2 and phase 3 trials.
L374: In subjects with UC, the model estimated mean apparent total clearance (CL/F) of etrasimod after oral administration was 1.06 L/h, mean apparent volume of distribution at steady state (Vss/F) was 70 L for a 70 kg subject, and the mean effective half-life (t1/2,eff) was 33.9 hours. PopPK estimated that subjects with UC would have slightly higher Cmax,ss and AUCss (about 10%) compared to healthy subjects of the same body weight, following 2 mg QD dosing. Hence, disease status is not expected to have a clinically significant effect on etrasimod PK exposure in subjects with UC.
L375: ## Pharmacodynamics
L376: In subjects with UC, mean lymphocyte counts decreased to approximately 50% of baseline at 2 weeks after treatment initiation and the lower lymphocyte counts were maintained during treatment. After discontinuing etrasimod, the median time for peripheral blood lymphocytes to return to the normal range was 2.6 weeks, with approximately 90% of subjects in the normal range within 4.7 weeks in phase 3 trials.
L377: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L378: ## General Dosing
L379: The recommended dosage regimen for adult patients with moderate to severe UC is 2 mg QD without regards of food.
L380: The effectiveness of etrasimod in adult patients was supported by the efficacy results from the phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302. A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg QD achieved clinical remission at Week 12 compared to placebo (27.4% versus 7.5% in APD334-301; 26.2% versus 15.2% in APD334-302) during the initial treatment period. Similarly, a greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg QD achieved clinical remission at Week 52 compared to placebo (32.5% versus 6.7%) in APD334-301. APD334-301 was a treat-through design in which all patients continued assigned treatment without rerandomization of responders following assessment of early response at Week 12.
L381: The Applicant has proposed a dosage of 2 mg QD in adults
L382: (b) (4)
L383: PK, clinical efficacy, and safety data are available from only 1 pediatric subject (16 years-old, female, body weight of 42 kg) received etrasimod 2 mg QD. Although adolescents aged 16 to less than 18 years of age were allowed to enroll in phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302, a total three adolescent patients were enrolled, and two subjects received placebo. No other clinical trials enrolled adolescent subjects.
L384: See Section 8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation of this multi-discipline review for the related efficacy and safety data.
L385: ## **Therapeutic Individualization**
L386: ## Renal Impairment
L387: No dose adjustment is needed in patients with renal impairment. Based on a dedicated renal impairment study, renal impairment has no clinically important effects on the PK of etrasimod. The effect of dialysis on etrasimod was not assessed.
L388: ## Hepatic Impairment
L389: No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A and B). Use of etrasimod in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) is not recommended based on 57% higher etrasimod exposure compared with subjects with normal liver function given multiple doses higher than 2 mg have not been studied.
L390: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L391: ## *Effects of Other Drugs on Etrasimod:*
L392: - In vitro data suggest that etrasimod is mainly metabolized via CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4, and with only minor contribution from CYP2C19 and CYP2J2.
- CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor alone: Only moderate effect of a strong inhibitor of a single CYP enzyme was observed. Overall, the risk for clinically relevant DDIs appears low with concomitant medications that inhibit only CYP2C8 or CYP3A4.**CYP3A4:** Itraconazole (a strong inhibitor of CYP3A4) had little impact on the C_{max} of etrasimod (increased by 6%) but increased AUC_{0-inf} of etrasimod by 32%.
- **CYP2C8:** Gemfibrozil (a strong inhibitor of CYP2C8) had little impact on the C_{max} of etrasimod (increased by 12%) but increased AUC_{0-inf} of etrasimod by 36%. Thus, no dose adjustment is warranted.
- Drugs that are both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 inhibitors: The AUC of etrasimod increased by 84% when co-administered with fluconazole (a moderate inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4, and strong inhibitor of CYP2C19). While fluconazole inhibits multiple CYP enzymes, it appears that the effect of fluconazole is mainly by inhibition of CYP2C9 given a strong CYP3A4
L393: ## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L394: inhibitor (itraconazole) increased AUC by 32%, although additive effects of moderate inhibition of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 cannot be ruled out. Thus, co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is a dual inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 (e.g., fluconazole) is not recommended.
L395: - Rifampin: The AUC of etrasimod decreased by 49% when co-administered with rifampin (a moderate inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 and a strong inducer of CYP2C19 and CYP3A4). Thus, co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is a combined CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate) inducer (i.e., rifampin) is not recommended.
L396: Additionally, administration of etrasimod in patients who are CYP2C9 poor metabolizers and receiving CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors is not recommended. In patients who are intermediate CYP2C9 metabolizers, the effect on etrasimod exposure when co-administered with CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is not known.
L397: ## *Effects of Etrasimod on Other Drugs:*
L398: ## DDI study with oral contraceptive:
L399: Co-administration of etrasimod with an oral contraceptive (containing 30 µg ethinylestradiol [EE] and 150 µg levonorgestrel [LVG]) increased C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau,ss} of EE by 12% and 24%, respectively, and increased C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau,ss} of LVG by 22% and 32%, respectively. These changes are not expected to be clinically relevant.
L400: No notable differences in mean concentration-time profiles for follicle stimulating hormone (FSH), luteinizing hormone (LH), estradiol, progesterone, and sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG), as well as the incidents of Hoogland scores were observed between when oral contraceptive was administered alone and co-administration of etrasimod and oral contraceptive. Steady-state plasma PK parameters for etrasimod appear generally consistent with previous findings for etrasimod when given alone, in a cross-study comparison. Thus, the review team agrees with the Applicant's proposal that no dosing adjustment is necessary when etrasimod is co-administered with oral contraceptive.
L401: No dosage adjustment based on other intrinsic or extrinsic factors is needed for adult subjects with UC. See details in Section 6.3.2.
L402: ## **Outstanding Issues**
L403: There are no outstanding issues that would preclude the approval of etrasimod for the currently proposed indication in adult patients with moderately to severely active UC from a clinical pharmacology perspective.
L404: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L405: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L406: The PK of etrasimod were characterized in healthy adult subjects and in subjects with UC. A summary of the general PK of etrasimod is provided in Table 12 below.

Distribution
Volume of distribution	The mean apparent volume of distribution of etrasimod (Vz/F) is 66 L.

Drug as substrate of transporters	Based on the in vitro studies, etrasimod is not a substrate or inhibitor of human ABC transporters (P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP), and SLC transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1/3, OCT1, OCT2, and MATE1/2 K).
Elimination
Mass balance results	
Fecal (hepatobiliary) excretion was the predominant route of elimination for etrasimod.
Following a single oral dose of radiolabeled etrasimod 2 mg, approximately 82% of the radioactivity was recovered in feces and 4.9% in urine over 336 hours.
Total administered radioactivity was recovered in urine and feces through the last collection interval (336 hours postdose) with 3.00% of the total administered radioactivity recovered in urine within the first 72 hours postdose and 57.2% of the total administered radioactivity recovered in feces by 144 hours postdose. Total of 11, 21, and 9 metabolites were observed in plasma, urine, and feces, respectively. In feces, metabolites M3 (AR503641) and M36 (APD334-oxy sulfate) accounted for a mean of 22.1% and 18.9% of the dose (total administered radioactivity), respectively, and unchanged etrasimod accounted for a mean of 11.2% of the dose. Unchanged etrasimod was not detected in urine.
No major circulating metabolites were observed. The two most abundant circulating metabolites of etrasimod, AR503641 (M3) and AR504344 (M6) accounted for <20% of the parent AUC as well as <10% of total radioactivity in human plasma.

Half-life	The mean plasma elimination t_{1/2} of etrasimod is approximately 30 hours.
Metabolic pathway(s)	Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple enzymes via oxidation, dehydrogenation, glucuronidation, sulfation and a combination of these reactions, though unchanged etrasimod is the main circulating component in plasma. In vitro assays indicate that etrasimod is mainly metabolized via CYP2C9, CYP2C8, and CYP3A4.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	Fecal excretion was the predominant route of excretion. See Mass balance results above.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	In population PK analysis, body weight was a significant covariate of etrasimod PK with lower etrasimod exposure associated with higher body weight. The C_{\text{trough,ss}} for subjects with body weights of 50 kg and 110 kg were estimated to be 1.20 and 0.779 times, respectively, that of a 70 kg subject.
Age/sex/race/ethnicity	The PK of etrasimod would not be expected to be significantly affected by age, sex, or race/ethnicity.
Renal impairment	Etrasimod C_{\text{max}} and AUC were comparable between subjects with severe renal impairment/ESRD and subjects with normal renal function.
Hepatic impairment	Etrasimod AUC parameters were 13%, 29%, and 57% higher in subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A, B, and C, respectively), respectively, compared with subjects with normal liver function.
Drug Interaction Liability Effects of Etrasimod on Other Drugs
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	
In vitro data suggested that the major human enzymes involved in the metabolism of etrasimod are CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4 and to a lesser extent CYP2C19 and CYP2J2.
Etrasimod (10 \mu\text{M}) was not a direct or time-dependent inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 or CYP3A4/5. Etrasimod is not an inducer of human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 based on the fold-change method as mRNA evaluations. Etrasimod was not an inhibitor of UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, or UGT2B17 in vitro.
No clinically meaningful effects of etrasimod on the steady-state C_{\text{max}} and AUC of oral contraceptive (30 \mu\text{g} ethinylestradiol and 150 \mu\text{g} levonorgestrel) were observed when co-administered with etrasimod 2 mg QD.

Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	In vitro studies suggest that etrasimod is not an inhibitor of human ABC transporters (P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP) or SLC transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1/3, OCT1, OCT2, and MATE1/2 K).

L413: Based on PopPK analysis, disease status is only expected to have a minimal effect on etrasimod PK exposure in subjects with UC. Healthy subjects have slightly smaller (within 10%) mean etrasimod C_{\text{max,ss}} and \text{AUC}_{\text{ss}} compared to UC patient of the same body weight, following 2 mg QD dosing.
L414: ## Heart Rate Reduction
L415: As a class, S1P1 agonists are known to affect heart rate especially following the first dose. A dose-dependent reduction in heart rate (HR) by etrasimod was observed with the maximum decline in HR from predose baseline observed from 2 to 4 hours postdose across clinical studies. Of note, median Tmax is 4 hours. Maximal mean HR decrease from baseline ranged from -3.3 to -19.4 bpm in healthy subjects following the first 2 mg dose of etrasimod. In subjects with UC, maximal mean HR change from baseline ranged from -7.3 to -9.4 bpm following the first 2 mg dose of etrasimod in phase 2 and 3 trials.
L416: Of note, several different etrasimod dose titration regimens were evaluated in phase 1 study APD334-110 and compared with dosing regimen without up-titration. Under dose titration regimens, i.e., initiating with a low (0.25-0.5 mg) starting dose followed by up-titration to 2 mg over a period of one week or less, as expected minimal HR-lowering effect was observed after the first dose on Day 1. The maximal HR-lowering effect was delayed and observed at 2 mg at the end of titration. Additionally, similar findings were also described using population PK/PD HR modeling as shown in Figure 3. The model estimated that while the 2 mg QD with titration regimen reduced the HR effect on the first day of treatment, the model predicted nadir, reached after 5 days of treatment, was similar to that of the 2 mg QD without titration regimen nadir reached on Day 1.
L417: As such, etrasimod dosages of 1 mg QD and 2 mg QD without dose titration were used in phase 2 trials and subsequently 2 mg QD without dose titration was used in phase 3 trials in subjects with UC.
L418: **Figure 3. Simulated Heart Rate in Subjects with UC Following 2 mg QD With vs. Without Dose Titration for 21 Days**
L419: Source: Applicant's Population PK/PD Report ARE0301H-HR, Figures 25 and 26.
L420: 2 mg QD without titration. for 21 days
L421: 2 mg QD with titration: 0.25 mg QD on Days 1 and 2, 0.5 mg on Day 3, 0.75 mg on Day 4, 1.25 mg on Day 5, then 2 mg QD on Days 6-21
L422: The solid lines represent the median. The shaded areas represent 50% (darker blue color), 75%, and 90% (lighter blue color) prediction intervals for data simulated under the model.
L423: On the other hand, when dose was titrated up from 2 mg to 4 mg (APD334-008 in healthy subjects: 2 mg on Day 1-7, 3 mg on Day 12, and 4 mg on Day 14), the HR lowering effect was greatest on Day 1 at 2 mg and subsequent dose increase to 3 mg and 4 mg did not enhance the effects as shown in Figure 4.

L426: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L427: ## Does the Clinical Pharmacology Program Provide Supportive Evidence of Effectiveness?
L428: The primary evidence of effectiveness of 2 mg etrasimod QD was supported by a significantly higher proportion of adult subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52 compared to placebo in the placebo-controlled phase 3 trials. See Section 8.1 of this multidisciplinary review for the related efficacy data.
L429: For adolescent subjects aged 16 years and older, there were very limited data from the phase 3 trials for this age group. Overall, there was only one pediatric subject that received etrasimod 2 mg QD in phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302.
L430: ## Is the Proposed Dosing Regimen Appropriate for the General Patient Population for Which the Indication is Being Sought?
L431: Yes. The proposed oral dosage of 2 mg QD for the general adult patient population is appropriate.
L432: ## Dose-Response Relationship
L433: In a phase 2 dose-ranging study APD334-003, etrasimod at 1 mg and 2 mg QD, or placebo, were studied in subjects with moderately to severely active UC for 12 weeks. Both doses showed numerically better efficacy based on the primary efficacy endpoint of the study (i.e., change from baseline in the modified Mayo score) compared to placebo at Week 12. Only the 2-mg dose was statistically significantly different from placebo for the primary efficacy endpoint. In addition, there was a dose-dependent increase in the proportion of subjects who achieved endoscopic improvement, and greater decrease from baseline in rectal bleeding and stool frequency subscores and the total Mayo score between 1 mg and 2 mg doses.
L434: The effects of etrasimod on lymphocyte counts were also assessed in this phase 2 study. Reduction of circulating lymphocyte counts was observed in both etrasimod dose groups at Week 12. Numerically greater reductions in lymphocyte counts from baseline were observed for the 2 mg dose compared to the 1 mg dose. The mean percent changes from baseline in absolute lymphocyte count (ALC) versus time profiles are presented in Figure 5. It should be noted that the clinical relevance of reduction in circulating lymphocyte counts with UC is not fully understood.
L435: **Figure 5. Mean (SE) Percent Change from Baseline in Lymphocyte Count**
L436: Source: Phase 2 trial APD334-003 report, Figure 5.
L437: Abbreviations: SE = standard error
L438: Although the data described above are supportive of the proposed 2 mg dose for induction, no dose exploration was performed for etrasimod for the treatment beyond week 12 in the phase 2 study and doses higher than 2 mg were not studied.
L439: As mentioned above, in the phase 3 trials, etrasimod 2 mg QD was supported by a significantly higher proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52. Refer to Section 8.1 for more details.
L440: ## Exposure-Response Relationship
L441: The exposure-response relationships for the observed rates of clinical remission at Week 12 and several Applicant-proposed secondary efficacy endpoints (i.e., endoscopic improvement, symptomatic remission, and mucosal healing)1 at Week 12 were explored using data from the phase 2 trial APD334-003 and the phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302 in adult subjects with UC using population PK/PD analysis, with individual PK parameter estimates from the final PopPK model. The exposure-response relationships are presented in Figure 6.
L442: **Figure 6. Exposure-Response Relationship for Predicted and Observed Probability of Efficacy Responses at Week 12 in Adult Subjects With UC**

L449: Applicant-proposed secondary endpoints (endoscopic improvement, symptomatic remission, and mucosal healing)1 at Week 12. There appeared to be a trend toward greater efficacy response with higher etrasimod exposure. Overall, exposure-response analysis estimated a higher proportion of patients achieving improvements in efficacy response in the etrasimod-treated group as compared to placebo.
L450: Of note, the overall incidence of treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) appeared to be higher in the etrasimod group (71.3%) compared to placebo (56.3%) in the phase 3 trial APD334-301, although most TEAEs were Grade 1 or Grade 2 in severity. The incidence of severe TEAEs, serious TEAEs, TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation were similar between the two treatment groups in the phase 3 trial APD334-301. Additionally, the overall incidence of TEAEs were similar between the etrasimod group (47.1%) and placebo (46.6%) in phase 3 trial APD334-302.
L451: Overall, the proposed dosage of 2 mg QD is considered appropriate for the treatment of adult patients with moderately to severely active UC based on the efficacy data in the phase 3 clinical trials. See Section 8 of this multidisciplinary review for the related efficacy and safety data.
L452: ## **Is an Alternative Dosing Regimen or Management Strategy Required for Subpopulations Based on Intrinsic Patient Factors?**
L453: Etrasimod is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic impairment. No dosage adjustment is needed based on other intrinsic factors.
L454: ## Hepatic Impairment
L455: Based on a dedicated hepatic impairment study, after single dose administration of 2 mg, mean C_{max} of etrasimod was generally similar between subjects with hepatic impairment and matched subjects with normal liver function; however, mean AUC_{0-inf} were 13%, 29%, and 57% higher in subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment, respectively, compared with subjects with normal liver function. Mean T_{1/2} increased as hepatic function decreased, ranging from 43.9 to 59.5 hours in normal matched controls and for 55.7 hours in subjects with mild hepatic impairment to 76.5 hours for subjects with severe hepatic impairment.
L456: The geometric mean AUC of M3 increased 3-fold and M6 increased 2-fold in the severe hepatic impairment group compared to matched-control.
L457: The summary of PK parameters for etrasimod is presented in Table 13.

L464: There was an unexpected decreasing trend in %fu with decreasing liver function (i.e., %fu of 4.35%, 3.84%, and 2.59% for subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment, respectively), while the mean %fu remained similar across the matched healthy control subjects (%fu ranging 4.67% to 5.11%). The unbound AUC0-inf in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (AUC0-inf,u of 70.8 ng·h/mL) appeared lower compared to that in matched subjects with normal liver function (AUC0-inf,u of 81.1 ng·h/mL). The reasons for this slight decreasing trend in %fu is unknown.
L465: Overall, the differences in AUC0-inf for subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment compared to healthy controls are not expected to be clinically meaningful to warrant dose adjustment. However, etrasimod is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic impairment, considering the extent of increase in AUC (57% higher) as well as lack of data from phase 3 trials on subjects with severe hepatic impairment.
L466: ## Renal Impairment
L467: Based on a dedicated renal impairment study, renal impairment does not have clinically significant effects on the PK of etrasimod. The mean Cmax and AUC of etrasimod were similar between subjects with severe renal impairment/end-stage renal disease (ESRD) and subjects with normal renal function as shown in Table 14. Of note, the effect of hemodialysis on the PK of etrasimod has not been evaluated.
