L472: ## Patients ≥65 Years of Age
L473: Based on population PK analysis, steady state PK estimates (Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss) of etrasimod in subjects over 65 years of age were similar (within 5%) to younger adult subjects. Thus, dose reduction for UC patients aged 65 years and older is deemed not necessary based on the available data.
L474: ## Body Weight
L475: Dose adjustment based on body weight is not needed for adult patients with UC.
L476: In population PK analysis, covariate effects for body weight were included on both clearance and volume terms. Model estimated the Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss values for etrasimod in a typical 50 kg subject are 125%, 123%, and 120%, respectively, of those from a reference 70 kg subject. Model predicted Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss values for etrasimod in a typical 110 kg subject are 74.1%, 75.7%, and 77.9%, respectively, of those from a reference 70 kg subject. Hence, the effect of body weight on etrasimod PK is not expected to be clinically meaningful to warrant dose adjustment across the typical body weight range in adults. Of note, body weight ranged 41.4 kg to 138 kg in subjects with UC who received 2 mg QD dosing in phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302.
L477: ## Pharmacogenetics
L478: Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple CYP enzymes in the liver. In vitro studies demonstrated that etrasimod was predominantly metabolized by CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP3A4, and with minor contribution via CYP2C19 and CYP2J2 (1% each).
L479: The CYP2C9 gene is highly polymorphic. Individuals could be categorized into normal metabolizers (NM), intermediate metabolizers (IM), and poor metabolizers (PM) based on CYP2C9 genotypes (*1, *2, or *3). The Applicant studied metabolism by using an in vitro recombinant CYP2C9 enzyme test. However, the Applicant did not submit a pharmacogenetic report or data for CYP2C9 genotypes from in vivo studies in the original submission package.
L480: The conversion of CYP2C9 genotype to phenotype relies on categorizing alleles into functional status based on assigned “activity value” ranging from 0-1 (e.g., 0 for no function, 0.5 for
L481: decreased function, and 1.0 for normal function), which are summarized into an “activity score (AS)” for each diplotype (see Table 15). The CYP2C9 phenotype frequencies differ across ancestrally diverse populations (see Table 16).
L482: **Table 15. Clinical Pharmacogenetics Implementation Consortium Standard for CYP2C9 Genotype to Phenotype Translation Using Activity Scoresa (AS)**
L483: Phenotypes	AS Definition	Examples of Diplotypes
Normal Metabolizer (NM)	2	*1/*1
Intermediate Metabolizer (IM)	1.5	*1/*2
1	*1/*3, *2/*2
Poor Metabolizer (PM)	0.5	*2/*3
0	*3/*3
L484: Source: Reviewer-generated table based on PMID: 32189324.
L485: Other no function and decreased function alleles are more prevalent in Non-White populations (e.g., *5, *6, *8, *11) than *2 and *3 alleles.
L486: a0 for no function, 0.5 for decreased function, and 1.0 for normal function
L487: **Table 16. Frequency of the CYP2C9 Phenotypes Across Racial/Ethnic Populations**
L488: Racial/Ethnic Populations	Phenotype Frequency (%) Based On AS
NM	IM	PM
African-American/Afro-Caribbean	76	24	0.1
American†	83	16	<0.1
Central/South Asian	60	36	3.8
East Asian	84	15	0.1
European	63	35	2.6
Latino	74	25	0.1

L489: Source: Reviewer-generated table based on https://cpicpgx.org/guidelines/cpic-guideline-for-nsaids-based-on-cyp2c9-genotype/Racial/Ethnic Population categorization based on PMID: 30506572.
L490: †The American genetic ancestry group includes populations from both North and South America with ancestors predating European colonization, including American Indian, Alaska Native, First Nations, Inuit, and Métis in Canada, and Indigenous peoples of Central and South America.
L491: Clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies with fluconazole (a moderate inhibitor of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4; study APD334-009) and gemfibrozil (a strong inhibitor of CYP2C8; study APD334-009) increased the AUC of etrasimod by 84% and 36%, respectively. Itraconazole (a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor; study APD334-116) increased the AUC of etrasimod by 32%. Rifampin (a strong inducer of CYP3A4 and moderate inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9; study APD334-009) decreased the AUC of etrasimod by 49%. Based on the results obtained from the DDI study with fluconazole (refer to additional study information below), a similar magnitude of increase in etrasimod exposure is expected for CYP2C9 poor metabolizers with co-administration of moderate or strong inhibitors of CYP2C8 or CYP3A4.
L492: As the results obtained from the DDI studies suggested a potential increase of exposure in subjects with genetically decreased CYP2C9 enzyme activity, an Information Request (IR) was sent to the Applicant (December 13, 2022) requesting available pharmacogenetic data for CYP2C9. According to the Applicant's response received on January 4, 2023, a CYP2C9 pharmacogenetic study was not conducted for the following reasons:
L493: - The clinical DDI data were consistent with the in vitro data, indicating involvement of three CYP enzymes and suggests that a single enzyme doesn't dominate the oxidative metabolism of etrasimod.
- Inter-subject variability across all the etrasimod PK studies was low to moderate (about 27% for CL/F based on a population PK analysis) with no evidence of outlying clusters of subjects with higher exposure.
- The treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were generally mild to moderate across phase 1 and UC studies.
L494: The review team agrees with the above rationales provided by the Applicant. However, considering the expected increase in etrasimod exposure for IM or PM CYP2C9 metabolizers with co-administration of CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors, an additional IR was sent on March 6, 2023, requesting recommendations for dosing approaches for patients who are CYP2C9 IMs or PMs and receiving CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors. The Applicant's response was received on March 20, 2023. The Applicant added the following language to the label, (b) (4)
L495: The proposed dose recommendation was based on the justification outlined below:
L496: - The Applicant conducted a review of the published literature on the impact of CYP2C9 allelic variants on in vivo drug clearance. For different CYP2C9 substrates, decreases in clearance in IMs relative to CYP2C9*1/*1 ranged from 3% (torsemide), 49% (celecoxib), and ~68% (S-warfarin). In PMs, clearances are 9% to 33% or as high as ~91% (S-warfarin in CYP2C9*3/*3 subjects), compared to the wild-type. The Applicant indicated that the observed range of impact on clearance for CYP2C9 IM phenotype with concomitant administration of an inhibitor of CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 is expected to result in &lt;50% decrease in etrasimod clearance (&lt;2-fold increase in AUC), relative to wild-type. Whereas in PMs, the decrease in clearance is unlikely to substantially exceed 50%, with an increase in exposure that is 2-fold or slightly higher.
- In the presence of fluconazole, while CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 pathways are moderately inhibited, a significant extent of CYP2C8 activity is available to metabolize etrasimod. Similarly, in IM or PM of CYP2C9 patients taking CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitor, the remaining uninhibited metabolic pathways are expected to be fully active. Therefore, the overall inhibition of metabolic activity of etrasimod in PM patients is not expected to substantially exceed 50%, compared to wild type.
- Based on the Applicant's population PK model, post hoc steady state AUC_{ss} values were generated for each subject receiving etrasimod 2 mg in the UC phase 3 trials. As shown in Figure 7, for the etrasimod single dose, AUC_{inf} in the presence of fluconazole (blue box plot) is within the distribution of AUC_{ss} (red box plot) as determined in subjects with
L497: UC from the phase 3 trials. According to the Applicant, this magnitude of increase in etrasimod AUC is not expected to be a significant safety concern.
L498: **Figure 7. Etrasimod AUC for Etrasimod Co-Administered with Fluconazole Compared to Predicted Exposure in UC Subjects Using a Population Pharmacokinetic Model**
L499: Source data: Left, Improve repository artifact: CP1:F1-40641155; Right, Module 5.3.3.4 Study APD334-009 CSR Listing 16.2.6.4
L500: Source: The Applicant PopPK analysis.
L501: The Applicant concluded that the increase in etrasimod exposure in patients with IM or PM CYP2C9 status taking concomitant moderate or strong CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is expected to be in a similar range as that observed in the fluconazole DDI study. Therefore, the recommendation for PM patients should be consistent with recommendation for fluconazole. Due to uncertainty in the range of potential changes in etrasimod exposure, the effect of etrasimod exposure with concomitant use of CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is unknown in CYP2C9 IM patients.
L502: In general, the review team agrees with the Applicant's justification. Pharmacogenomic labeling recommendations will be included in the etrasimod label.
L503: ## Are There Clinically Relevant Food-Drug or Drug-Drug Interactions, and What is the Appropriate Management Strategy?
L504: The proposed dosing instruction to administer etrasimod with or without food is appropriate.
L505: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L506: - Co-administration of etrasimod with drugs that are both moderate CYP2C9 and moderate or strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., fluconazole) is not recommended.
- Co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is a combined CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate) inducer (e.g., rifampin) is not recommended.
- Co-administration of etrasimod with drugs that are CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor in patients who are known to be CYP2C9 poor metabolizers is not recommended. In patients who are intermediate metabolizers of CYP2C9 substrates, the effect on etrasimod exposure when co-administered with CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is not known.
L507: ## Food Effect
L508: The effect of food on etrasimod PK was evaluated in study APD334-114 using the proposed TBM formulation. No significant food effect on the PK of etrasimod was observed when a single dose of 2 mg etrasimod was administered with a high-fat meal as compared to the fasted state. Geometric mean C_{max} and AUC were similar between the fed state and the fasted state (Table 17). Mean T_{max} was delayed by 2 hours in the fed state (6 h) compared to the fasted state (4 h).

L513: ## DDI Study with Fluconazole, Gemfibrozil, and Rifampin
L514: A clinical drug interaction study (APD334-009) was conducted to evaluate the effects of fluconazole, gemfibrozil, or rifampin on the PK of etrasimod in healthy adult subjects.
L515: Co-administration of fluconazole 200 mg QD (400 mg on the first day, followed by 200 mg QD for 14 days) with a single dose of etrasimod 1 mg resulted in an increase of 12% in mean C_{max} and an increase of 84% in mean AUC0-inf of etrasimod as shown in Table 18.

L532: Considering in vitro data suggested that etrasimod is metabolized by multiple CYP enzymes, the risk for clinically relevant DDIs appears low with concomitant medications that inhibit only a single enzymatic pathway. Overall, the increase in systemic exposure (up to 36%) is not considered clinically significant to warrant dose adjustment when etrasimod is co-administered with a CYP2C8 inhibitor.
L533: ## DDI Study with Itraconazole
L534: A clinical drug interaction study (APD334-116) was conducted to evaluate the effects of itraconazole on the PK of etrasimod in healthy adult subjects.
L535: Co-administration of itraconazole (a strong inhibitor of CYP3A4) 200 mg QD for 13 days with a single dose of etrasimod 1 mg had no or minimum impact on the mean C_{max} (6% increase) but resulted in an increase of 32% in mean AUC_{0-inf} of etrasimod as shown in Table 21.

L541: ## DDI Study with Oral Contraceptive
L542: Study APD334-111 was conducted to evaluate the effects of etrasimod on the PK and PD of an oral contraceptive (30 µg ethinylestradiol [EE] and 150 µg levonorgestrel [LVG]) in healthy premenopausal female subjects.
L543: Co-administration of etrasimod 2 mg QD with the oral contraceptives (OCs) QD at steady state increased the mean Cmax of EE by 12% and increased the mean AUC0-inf by 24%. For LVG, co-administration of etrasimod 2 mg QD resulted in an increase of the mean Cmax by 22% and an increase of the mean AUC0-inf by 32% as shown in Table 22.

L548: Overall, no clinically meaningful effects of etrasimod on the steady-state Cmax or AUC of EE and LVG were observed when co-administered with etrasimod 2 mg QD.
L549: In addition to the PK assessment, the effect of etrasimod on PD measures of ovarian activity (i.e., follicle stimulating hormone (FSH), luteinizing hormone (LH), estradiol, and progesterone, sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG), and transvaginal ultrasound (TVUS)/Hoogland scores) were explored in this study. Graphical assessment suggested that co-administration of etrasimod and OC did not show clinically meaningfully altered hormone concentration levels of FSH, LH, estradiol, and progesterone compared to OC administration alone. A similar increasing trend in SHBG levels was observed in both OC administration alone and co-administration of etrasimod and OC treatment, which is expected for monophasic OC containing both EE and LVG (Abdalla, Shabaan, and Stanczyk 1992). The incidents of Hoogland scores also showed similar trends for both OC treatments.
L550: ## Smoking Status
L551: Population PK analysis estimated that steady state PK estimates (C_{max,ss}, AUC_{ss}, and C_{trough,ss}) of etrasimod in a typical tobacco user were similar (within 2%) to those from a reference male non-user of tobacco of the same body weight.
L552: ## Immune System Effects After Stopping Etrasimod
L553: As lymphocyte count is reported to return to the normal range in 90% of subjects within 4 to 5 weeks of stopping etrasimod in subjects with UC, use of immunosuppressants within this period may lead to an additive effect on the immune system. Monitoring of patients receiving concomitant immunosuppressants for infectious complications during treatment and for 5 weeks after the last dose of etrasimod will be recommended in the label.
L554: ## **Bridge Between the “To-Be-Marketed” and Clinical Trial Formulations**
L555: Since the formulation used in the phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302 is different from the to-be-marketed formulation (2 mg oral tablets), a relative bioavailability study (APD334-114) was conducted to bridge the clinical trial formulation to the to-be-marketed formulation. Bioequivalence criteria were met for systemic exposures between these two formulations. Geometric mean C_{max} and AUC were comparable between the formulation used in the phase 3 trials and the to-be-marketed formulation as shown in Table 23.

L560: A request to conduct an inspection of the clinical and bioanalytical sites for Study APD334-114 was submitted to the Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance (OSIS). OSIS has conducted the inspection of the both clinical and bioanalytical sites for Study APD334-114 and recommends accepting the study data for review (see OSIS Memos by Dr. Gajendiran Mahadevan dated June 2, 2023 [DARRTS Reference ID: 5183704], and by Dr. Melkamu Getie Kebtie dated June 27, 2023 [DARRTS Reference ID: 5197761]).

L569: ## 7.2. Review Strategy
L570: The clinical development program for etrasimod that supported the determination of safety and effectiveness included one 12-week phase 2 trial (APD334-003), one 12-week phase 3 trial (APD334-302), and one 52-week phase 3 trial (APD334-301).
L571: ## Efficacy
L572: The determination of effectiveness was based on the two phase 3 trials, APD334-301 and APD334-302. No pooling of data across trials was conducted for the assessment of efficacy. The two trials are summarized as follows:
L573: - **APD334-301:** A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. The trial was a “treat-through” design for a duration of 52 weeks with an initial treatment period of 12 weeks, at which time endpoints were assessed (i.e., Week 12). Subjects then continued their blinded, randomized treatment for an additional 40 weeks, at which time endpoints were assessed (i.e., Week 52). After Week 12, subjects who met the prespecified definition of UC worsening2 were eligible to leave APD334-301 and enter the open-label extension study (APD334-303).
- **APD334-302:** A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. The trial duration was 12 weeks.
L574: During the review, several issues were identified which resulted in the primary efficacy analyses being conducted in a redefined population (“FDA Preferred Analysis Population”) as explained below.
L575: ## Disease Severity Criteria
L576: APD334-301 and APD334-302 defined moderately to severely active UC as a modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 4 to 9, including an endoscopic subscore (ES) of at least 2 and rectal bleeding subscore (RBS) of at least 1. Prior to completion of the Applicant’s phase 3 program, the Division communicated to the Applicant that the Division’s recommended definition of moderately to severely active UC includes subjects with a mMS of 5 to 9 and an ES of at least 2. The Division raised concerns that a mMS of 4 may result in the enrollment of subjects with mild symptoms (e.g., a subject with a SFS of 1, RBS of 1, and ES of 2), in whom achieving the
L577: 2 Subjects with disease worsening were defined as having at least 1 of the following criteria: 1) absolute rectal bleeding subscore (RBS) \ge 2 on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of endoscopic subscore (ES) \ge 2, 2) absolute RBS + stool frequency subscore (SFS) \ge 4 on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of ES \ge 2 or 3) absolute RBS \ge 2 or RBS + SFS \ge 4 (in any order) on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of ES \ge 2.
L578: definition of remission (which may include a SFS of 1) may not represent a clinically meaningful improvement. The Applicant performed the requested additional analyses using the Division's recommended definition of moderately to severely active UC, and the results of analyses in this population are the focus of this multidisciplinary review.
L579: ## Pediatric Subjects
L580: APD334-301 and APD334-302 enrolled subjects 16 to 80 years of age inclusive, (b) (4)
L581: However, only 1 adolescent subject (who received placebo) was enrolled in APD334-301 and only 2 adolescent subjects (1 placebo, 1 etrasimod) were enrolled in APD334-302. Therefore, given data were available for only one adolescent subject who received etrasimod, the Division communicated to the Applicant (b) (4)
L582: the results of analyses in adults are the focus of this multidisciplinary review. The efficacy populations used in this review are shown in Table 25.
L583: **Table 25. Efficacy Populations APD334-301 and APD334-302**
L584: Population	Definition	Number of Subjects
APD334-301	Number of Subjects

L586: ## **Safety**
L587: The primary safety analyses were performed on the 52-week trial APD334-301, and on pooled safety data from the 12-week trials APD334-302 and APD334-003. The pooling of the data from APD334-302 and APD334-003 for subjects that received the same dose of etrasimod or placebo was appropriate as the study design, demographics, baseline disease characteristics, and prior and concomitant therapy were similar between the two trials. Pooling of the 12-week trials provided an assessment of adverse events in a larger safety database, as compared to evaluating each study individually.
L588: The safety of etrasimod was assessed in the safety population, which included all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of study treatment. For trials APD334-301 and APD334-302, the safety population is the same as the FAS because all randomized subjects received at least one dose of study treatment.
L589: Additionally, The Applicant submitted pooled safety data from the integrated summary of safety (ISS). The ISS consisted of safety data from the placebo-controlled parent studies
L590: APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003, as well as their open-label extension studies APD334-303 (for APD334-301 and APD334-302) and APD334-005 (for APD334-003). The ISS also included data from the open-label portion of an ongoing placebo-controlled trial conducted in East Asia, ES101002. The open-label extension studies are described below.
L591: - Safety Information from Open-Label Studies**ES101002:** A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in East Asia. This trial was ongoing at the time of the NDA submission; however, data from the open-label portion of the trial were included. Subjects that were nonresponders at Week 12 and those that experienced disease worsening during ES101002 were eligible to enter the open-label treatment period with a treatment duration of up to 40 weeks.
- **APD334-005:** An open-label extension study for subjects that completed APD334-003. All subjects who completed APD334-003 and met the eligibility criteria for the APD334-005 extension study had the option to enroll and receive open-label etrasimod 2 mg once daily for 34 weeks, which was later extended to 40 weeks under Protocol Amendment 2.
- **APD334-303:** An open-label extension study for subjects that completed APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-308. Subjects from the ongoing studies APD334-210 (a 52-week study in moderately active UC) and APD334-203 (a 12-week study in Japanese subjects with moderately to severely active UC) were also eligible to enroll in APD334-303. All subjects received open-label etrasimod 2 mg once daily for up to 5 years.
L592: Given the open-label nature of the data from ES101002, APD334-005, and APD334-303, results were not considered to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. Additionally, APD334-308 was not submitted as part of the original NDA submission and consisted of summary safety information; as such, data from APD334-308 was not considered to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. However, safety data were reviewed to assess for rare events given the extended duration of the open-label studies.
L593: ## 8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
L594: ## 8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L595: ## 8.1.1. APD334-301
L596: ## Trial Design
L597: APD334-301 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg over 52 weeks. The trial population included subjects with a modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 4 to 9 who had an inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1 of the following: oral 5-aminosalicylic acid (5-ASA) compounds, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologic therapy (TNF blocker, vedolizumab, anti-interleukin [anti-IL] 12/23), or Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors. A total of 433 subjects, including 1 adolescent, from 37 countries were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 52 weeks. The trial included an initial 12-week treatment period, after which, subjects who met the predefined definition for UC worsening2 were eligible to enter into the open-label extension trial (APD334-303). All other subjects were to continue on their randomized treatment for an additional 40 weeks. Subjects that met the predefined definition for UC worsening during this 40-week treatment period could switch to the open-label extension trial. Randomization was stratified by prior treatment status (i.e., naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry [yes/no]), baseline corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]). The overall trial design for APD334-301 is shown in Figure 8.
L598: **Figure 8. Trial Design for APD334-301**
L599: Source: Applicant's clinical study report for APD334-301, Figure 1 (page 22).
L600: Abbreviation: JAK, Janus kinase; UC, ulcerative colitis
L601: ## Trial Endpoints
L602: The co-primary efficacy endpoints comparing etrasimod to placebo consisted of:
L603: - The proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12
- The proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 52
L604: Clinical remission was evaluated using the mMS and was defined as a stool frequency subscore (SFS) of 0 or a SFS of 1 with a \ge 1 point decrease from baseline, rectal bleeding subscore (RBS) of 0, and endoscopic subscore (ES) of 0 or 1 (excluding friability). The Applicant's definition of clinical remission differed from the Agency's preferred definition of clinical remission of SFS of 0 or 1 (i.e., no requirement for a \ge 1 point decrease from baseline), RBS of 0, and ES of 0 or 1 (excluding friability). The multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints and definitions are shown in Table 26 and were subject to overall type I error control (tested based on a gatekeeping approach). Refer to Section 16.4.1 for additional details on the multiple testing procedure.
L605: **Table 26. Multiplicity-Controlled Secondary Efficacy Endpoints for Trial APD334-301**
L606: Secondary Endpoint	Definition
Endoscopic improvement at Week 12	ES \le 1 (excluding friability)
Endoscopic improvement at Week 52	ES \le 1 (excluding friability)
Symptomatic remission at Week 12	SFS = 0 (or = 1 with a \ge 1 point decrease from baseline) and RBS of 0
Symptomatic remission at Week 52	SFS = 0 (or = 1 with a \ge 1 point decrease from baseline) and RBS of 0
Corticosteroid-free clinical remission at Week 52	Clinical remission at Week 52 and who had not been receiving corticosteroids for \ge 12 weeks prior to Week 52
Mucosal healing at Week 12	ES of \le 1 (excluding friability) with histologic remission measured by a Geboes Index score <2.0
Mucosal healing at Week 52	ES of \le 1 (excluding friability) with histologic remission measured by a Geboes Index score <2.0
Sustained clinical remission at Week 52	Clinical remission at both Week 12 and Week 52
L607: Source: Review team's table
L608: Abbreviation: ES, endoscopic subscore; SFS, stool frequency subscore; RBS, rectal bleeding subscore
L609: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L610: ## Populations
L611: The Applicant's primary efficacy analyses were based on subjects in the full analysis set (FAS) with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 including an ES of at least 2. The FAS consisted of all randomized subjects who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment.
L612: The single adolescent subject was excluded from the efficacy analysis population, resulting in the "FDA Preferred Analysis Population." The Division's efficacy analyses (in addition to confirming the Applicant's analyses) focus on efficacy in the FDA preferred analysis population, defined as all adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 including an ES of at least 2. The safety population included all randomized subjects (including adolescents and
L613: subjects with mMS of 4) who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment, which is used for safety-related analysis.
L614: ## Sample Size
L615: The trial was powered based on assumed clinical remission rates at Week 52 of 23.5% for etrasimod versus 10% for placebo. A sample size of 420 (280 etrasimod, 140 placebo) was expected to provide 93.4% power to demonstrate that etrasimod is superior to placebo in achieving the clinical remission at Week 52, as assessed using a 2-group Fisher's exact test with a 1-sided significance level of 0.025. With this sample size, there was 96% power to detect a difference of 12.5% in the other primary endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12, assuming a placebo rate of 6%. The lower bound of overall power for both primary endpoints were at least 90%.
L616: ## Calculation of SFS and RBS
L617: The coprimary efficacy endpoints were based on the mMS which contains three subscore components: SFS, RBS, and ES. On visits when the mMS was calculated, SFS and RBS were derived by averaging the scores from the 3 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days prior to the day of bowel preparation (excluding the day of bowel preparation) and rounding to the nearest integer. On visits without endoscopy, SFS and RBS were derived by averaging the scores from the 3 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days prior to the date of visit and rounding to the nearest integer. If 3 consecutive days were not available, then the average of the 2 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days would be used. Sensitivity analyses were conducted that considered subjects with only 2 consecutive days available as non-responders. The outcomes of these analyses were consistent with the primary efficacy results.
L618: ## Analyses of Efficacy Endpoints
L619: For assessments of the coprimary endpoints and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, the estimated difference in proportions between arms and the corresponding 95% confidence interval (CI) were computed based on the stratified risk difference using the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) weights. The analyses adjusted for the following randomization stratification factors: naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (yes/no), baseline corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]).
L620: ## Estimands and Intercurrent Events
L621: The estimand corresponding to the co-primary efficacy endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12 evaluated the difference in the proportion of subjects in clinical remission at Week 12 for etrasimod 2 mg versus placebo in subjects with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 in the FAS population. For the intercurrent events of 1) premature treatment discontinuation for worsening of disease, lack of efficacy, or adverse event related to UC, 2) initiation of a rescue medication for UC, 3) an increase in dose over baseline levels in subjects' existing UC medication, or 4) rescue medical procedure during the trial, subjects were considered non-responders. The other co-
L622: primary endpoint at Week 52 and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints used the same estimand as the co-primary endpoint at Week 12.
L623: ## Handling of Missing Data
L624: For analyses of the coprimary efficacy endpoints and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, missing data were imputed using non-responder imputation (NRI). Sensitivity analyses for all multiplicity-controlled endpoints included multiple imputation under missing at random (MAR), tipping point analysis, multiple imputation with Copy Reference (CR) under missing not at random (MNAR), and a hybrid imputation approach with multiple imputation used to handle missing endoscopy data due to the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, and NRI for missing data not due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
L625: ## Multiplicity Adjustment
L626: A gatekeeping multiple testing procedure was specified to control the overall Type I error rate at 2-sided alpha of 0.05 for all coprimary endpoints and major secondary endpoints. Refer to Figure 29 in the Appendix Section 16.4.1 for the details on the gatekeeping procedure.
L627: ## Protocol Amendments
L628: The original study protocol for APD334-301 had four amendments. A summary of changes for each amendment are described in Table 27.
L629: Table 27. Summary of Changes for APD334-301 Protocol Amendments
L630: Amendment	Date	Summary of Changes
1.0	March 05, 2019	Additional procedures were added to the cardiac monitoring required on Day 1 after the first dose of study treatment. Post-dose safety vital signs, 12-lead ECGs, monitoring of subjects who do not meet the discharge criteria, and discontinuation rules were revised to align with FDA feedback.

2.0	December 20, 2019	Criteria for early (i.e., prior to Week 52) enrollment in the open-label study and eligibility criteria (e.g., list of prior therapy failures or non-response, contraception use, cardiovascular disease history, and prior therapy washout period) were updated.
Additional instructions for safety monitoring related to 12-lead ECG, pulmonary function tests (PFTs), and ophthalmoscopy and optical coherence tomography (OCT) testing were added.
Further instructions for QuantiFERON TB Gold and tuberculin skin test were added.
A 4-Week Follow-Up visit was added.

3.0	February 7, 2020	Clarification and further instructions for tuberculosis testing and an update for the tuberculosis screening questionnaire was made.
A correction was made to the entry criteria for enrollment in the OLE study.
Lack of efficacy was added as a reason a subject may discontinue from double-blind treatment.
Clarifications were made to ophthalmoscopy and OCT testing.

4.0	December 22, 2020	Guidance for conducting virtual and offsite visits was updated in the protocol.
Information on anti-arrhythmic drugs was added to prohibited concomitant therapies
The corticosteroid-free remission secondary endpoint was updated to evaluate corticosteroid-free remission in all subjects rather than those on corticosteroids at baseline.
Language was updated regarding timing of screening OCTs and PFTs.
An exclusion criterion was added regarding treatment with topical rectal Chinese medicine, enemas or suppositories prior to randomization.
L631: None of the changes had a major impact on the overall study design, efficacy assessments, or negatively impacted collection of safety data.
L632: ## 8.1.2. Trial Results (APD334-301)
L633: ## Patient Disposition
L634: The “FDA Preferred Analysis Population” consisted of a total of 408 subjects, 274 in the etrasimod 2 mg group and 134 in the placebo group; 55% (n=150) in the etrasimod group and
L635: 28% (n=38) in the placebo group completed the trial. The disposition of subjects in the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population” was similar to the FAS.
L636: In both the placebo and etrasimod groups, the most frequently reported reasons for trial discontinuation were disease worsening, withdrawal by subject, and adverse events. Table 28 summarizes subject disposition by treatment group.

L644: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L645: A total of 212 (52%) subjects had at least 1 important protocol deviation, including 145 (53%) subjects in the etrasimod group and 67 (50%) subjects in the placebo group. The most frequently reported (&gt; 5% subjects) important protocol deviations by category were “Study Procedures” (e.g., tests were not performed during the trial due to COVID-19, subjects did not return IP bottle due to non-compliance), “Laboratory Assessment”, “Eligibility and Entry Criteria” (e.g., tests were not performed during screening visit due to COVID-19, subjects were randomized into the trial despite meeting exclusion criteria due to an oversight), Concomitant Medication” (e.g., baseline corticosteroid dose was changed, corticosteroids were not tapered after Week 12), and “Visit Schedule.” The most common protocol deviations in both cohorts were “Study Procedures and Laboratory Assessment”, followed by “Eligibility and Entry Criteria” in the etrasimod group, and “Concomitant medication” in the placebo group. The number and distribution of protocol deviations were similar across both groups.
L646: ## Table of Demographic Characteristics
L647: The demographic characteristics are summarized by treatment group in Table 29. Overall, the
L648: majority of patients were male (53% and 59% in etrasimod and placebo groups, respectively) and most were White (89% in both treatment groups). The mean age at consent was approximately 42 years and 39 years for etrasimod and placebo groups, respectively, with a range in ages from 18-78 years for both groups. The mean BMI was 25kg/m2 in both groups, ranging from 13kg/m2 to 51kg/m2 in the etrasimod group, and 16kg/m2 to 50 kg/m2 in the placebo group. Demographic characteristics were generally similar between treatment groups.
L649: It is notable that most subjects were enrolled outside the U.S. However, as there are no well-established differences in UC disease severity or response to treatment by different ethnic groups, and the disease activity scores and documentation of prior treatment failure were standardized by the protocol, it is reasonable to generalize the result to the U.S. population.

L665: ## **Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use**
L666: Overall compliance was calculated as the total number of tablets taken divided by the total number of tablets expected during the treatment period. Subjects who took less than 80% or more than 120% of investigational drug during the entire treatment period were considered non-compliant. Treatment compliance was 98% for the etrasimod and placebo groups, respectively.
L667: Subjects must have had inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1 prior approved therapy for UC, including oral 5-ASAs, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologics, and JAK inhibitors. Oral 5-ASA, AZA, 6-MP, oral corticosteroids, or medicinal probiotics were permitted at screening and as per the inclusion criteria (Trial APD334-301 Protocol Section 4.1). Concomitant treatments for UC were required to be stable prior to and during the trial and
L668: could not be initiated during screening or the treatment periods. Immunosuppressive agents such as oral AZA and 6-MP were to be discontinued \ge 2 weeks prior to randomization. The majority of subjects received at least 1 concomitant medication for UC during the trial (89% in the etrasimod group and 88% in the placebo group), the majority of which were oral 5-ASAs.
L669: ## Efficacy Results – Co-Primary Endpoints
L670: A total of 433 subjects were in the FAS population, among which 409 subjects had baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2. The Applicant's pre-specified primary analysis was performed on subjects in the FAS population with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2, which included one adolescent subject.
L671: The adolescent subject was excluded from the efficacy analysis population, resulting in a total of 408 subjects (i.e., the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population”). Unless otherwise specified, all efficacy results in this review are based on the FDA Preferred Analysis Population defined as all adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2.
L672: The Applicant's definition of clinical remission was different from the FDA preferred definition of clinical remission. Refer to Section 8.1.1 for details. The review team recommended the FDA preferred definition for assessment of the coprimary endpoint and for labeling, because the definition corresponds to the Agency's currently recommended definition of clinical remission as described in the draft guidance for industry *Ulcerative Colitis: Developing Drugs for Treatment* (April 2022).3 Unless otherwise specified, the FDA preferred definition of clinical remission is used in all applicable efficacy analyses.
L673: On December 18, 2022, FDA sent an information request to the Applicant, asking the Applicant to provide new analyses on all multiplicity-controlled endpoints based on adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2, and based on the FDA preferred definition of clinical remission. On Jan 11, 2023, the updated results were submitted and verified by the review team.
L674: The primary efficacy results demonstrate superiority of etrasimod 2 mg compared to placebo in achieving clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52, as summarized in Table 31. The estimated difference between etrasimod compared to placebo in the percentage of subjects achieving clinical remission was 20.1% at Week 12 and 25.7% at Week 52, with p-values less than 0.0001 for both comparisons.
L675: The percentage of subjects with missing data was 9.9% for clinical remission at Week 12 and 44.5% for clinical remission at Week 52 in the etrasimod 2 mg group. The percent missing was 11.2% for clinical remission at Week 12 and 73.1% for clinical remission at Week 52 in the
L676: 3 When final, this guidance will represent the FDA's current thinking on this topic. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents.
L677: placebo group. For the endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12, most of the missing data were due to trial discontinuation, with the main reasons for discontinuation being adverse events or withdrawal by subject. For clinical remission at Week 52, most of the missing data were due to trial discontinuation with the main reason for discontinuation being disease worsening in both arms. A higher dropout rate was observed in the placebo arm compared to the etrasimod 2 mg arm. A non-responder imputation approach was used to handle missing data regardless of the reason for dropout. As UC is a chronic condition and etrasimod is intended as a chronic treatment, subjects who prematurely discontinued treatment would not experience a long-term benefit from the trial treatment. Therefore, non-responder imputation was considered appropriate for handling missing data due to dropout.

L712: ## Discussion of Primary and Secondary Endpoints Proposed for Labeling
L713: ## Clinical Remission
L714: The Applicant's proposed definition of clinical remission differs from the FDA preferred definition of clinical remission due to the requirement for subjects to demonstrate at least 1-point decrease from baseline in the SFS. The efficacy results for clinical remission using the FDA preferred definition and the Applicant's definition were similar and both were statistically significant. To align with the Division's current thinking, the team concluded that the efficacy endpoint of clinical remission based on the FDA preferred definition will be included in the label to replace the Applicant's prespecified coprimary endpoints of clinical remission (which
L715: includes a requirement of at least 1-point decrease from baseline in the SFS).
L716: ## Endoscopic Improvement
L717: The Applicant's proposed definition of endoscopic improvement aligns with the Division's recommended definition. Achievement of resolution of gross inflammation that can be visualized on endoscopy is a clinically relevant outcome of interest, as patients who achieve endoscopic improvement have been shown to have better outcomes than those with continued active inflammation. Therefore, this endpoint will be included in labeling.
L718: ## Symptomatic Remission
L719: The Applicant defined symptomatic remission as a SFS of 0 (or 1 with at least 1-point decrease from baseline), and RBS of 0 on the mMS. Therefore, symptomatic remission is the same as clinical remission (based on the Applicant's definition) without the endoscopy assessment. There is no consensus on the definition of the term "symptomatic remission" and the concept is redundant to the primary endpoint given that improvement in stool frequency and rectal bleeding are incorporated in clinical remission. Therefore, the endpoint of symptomatic remission will not be included in labeling. However, there was a trend toward more subjects experiencing improvement in SFS at Week 2 and RBS at Week 4. This information is useful to clinicians to help characterize the anticipated onset of improvement in symptoms. Therefore, this information will be included descriptively in labeling. Refer to the Discussion of Additional Endpoints below.
L720: ## Histologic-Endoscopic Mucosal Improvement
L721: The endpoint definition for "mucosal healing" represents an important clinical concept, incorporating both improvement of the endoscopic appearance of the mucosa (ES \le1 excluding friability) and improvement in histologic abnormalities, including absence of any neutrophilic inflammation (Geboes score &lt;2.0). This combination represents an important clinical endpoint and will be reflected in labeling. However, there is no standard definition for "mucosal healing" and the definition varies depending on the source. There are also uncertainties regarding the assessment of the histologic component of this endpoint, including the potential limitation of obtaining biopsies from a single segment of the colon, as the degree of histologic inflammation may vary by location of the biopsies. Although improvement in histologic findings may be observed in the location from which the biopsy is taken, there is uncertainty as to whether other locations in the colon have also improved. Therefore, the endpoint will be described in labeling as histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement, rather than "mucosal healing."
L722: The Applicant utilized a definition of ES of 0 or 1 (excluding friability) and a Geboes score of &lt;2.0, which represents no neutrophils in the epithelial crypts or lamina propria, no increase in eosinophils, and no crypt destruction, erosions, ulcerations, or granulation tissue.
L723: The Applicant proposed that the clinical relevance of this definition was supported by literature. For example, the Applicant described that the improvement, and resolution, of
L724: ## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L725: inflammation in UC, as measured by a histologic assessment and endoscopic measures [respectively], compared to endoscopic measures alone, is associated with lower rates of clinical relapse, corticosteroid use, hospitalization, and colectomy. Furthermore, the Applicant stated that a "using very conservative standards (i.e., Geboes &lt; 2.0, indicating neutrophil absence in the epithelium and no increase detected in the lamina propria) is supported by International Consensus and Position Papers."
L726: Although the review team agrees with this justification, there were some important limitations to the histologic evaluation in this program. Thirty-three subjects (approximately 8%) entered the trial with a Geboes score of &lt; 2.0 on the baseline biopsy, thus meeting the histologic component of the endpoint definition. Therefore, it was unclear whether achieving histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement represents an improvement for subjects with a Geboes score of &lt; 2.0 at baseline. The review team conducted a sensitivity analysis in which this endpoint was reanalyzed only in the subgroup of subjects with a baseline Geboes score of \ge 2.0. Results were consistent with those obtained in the FDA preferred analysis population, and therefore the team concluded that it is reasonable to include this endpoint in the label.
L727: Additionally, although investigators were instructed to perform biopsies on the mucosa "most representative of the inflammation seen in the region," there may be variation in histologic findings due to sampling. UC is generally considered to involve continuous segments of the GI tract; however, discontinuous patterns of inflammation may also be observed, particularly in subjects with a prior history of treatment (Bernstein et al. 1995). Furthermore, the clinical significance of achieving "histologic improvement" (in addition to endoscopic improvement) has not been well characterized and is limited by the various definitions used in the literature to define this concept (Battat et al. 2019). Due to these limitations, the label will indicate that the relationship between any histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement endpoint and disease progression and longer-term outcomes beyond 52 weeks has not been assessed.
L728: ## Discussion of Additional Endpoints
L729: ## Clinical Response
L730: Clinical response at Week 12 is a clinically meaningful endpoint. Although clinical response at Week 12 was not multiplicity-controlled, the etrasimod group had a numerically higher proportion of clinical responders in comparison with those treated with placebo, with an observed p-value less than 0.001. Clinical response at Week 52 is less clinically important than clinical response at Week 12 as subjects were expected to achieve clinical remission at Week 52, which has a more stringent definition. Therefore, language describing only the results of clinical response at Week 12 will be included in labeling.
L731: ## Stool Frequency and Rectal Bleeding Subscores
L732: Individual analyses of the SFS and RBS of the mMS in APD334-301 showed evidence that subjects treated with etrasimod reported symptomatic relief after only a few weeks of dosing (Figure 9 and Figure 10). Subjects in the etrasimod treatment group showed a nominally
L733: ## NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
## Velsipity (etrasimod)
L734: significant and greater mean reduction in SFS as early as Week 2 (nominal p-value = 0.0258) and in RBS as early as Week 4 (nominal p-value = 0.0007) compared to the placebo group based on the estimates from a mixed-effect model with repeated measures (MMRM). The improvement from baseline in SFS and RBS was sustained in the etrasimod group starting from Week 20.

L741: ## Endoscopic Remission
L742: The Applicant's definition of endoscopic remission is consistent with the Division's preferred definition of endoscopic remission. Although the endoscopic remission endpoint was not multiplicity-controlled, this endpoint demonstrated a nominally significant effect and represents a clinically relevant endpoint that is informative to prescribers as part of their clinical practice. Therefore, the results of endoscopic remission (at Week 12 and both Weeks 12 and 52) will be included descriptively in labeling.
L743: ## Endoscopic Remission and Geboes Histologic Score &lt; 2.0
L744: This endpoint is similar to histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement but included a more stringent definition of resolution of endoscopic findings, characterized by normal appearing mucosa (ES = 0). However, this endpoint was not pre-specified in the protocol or in the SAP. Therefore, this endpoint will not be included in labeling.
L745: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
L746: ## Bowel Urgency and Abdominal Pain
L747: Bowel urgency is a common symptom of UC and is associated with reduced quality of life (Dubinsky et al. 2022). The Applicant assessed bowel urgency severity with the Urgency Numeric Rating Scale (NRS) patient-reported outcome (PRO) measure, a single-item PRO measure assessing severity of a subject's urgency (the sudden or immediate need) to have a bowel movement within the past 24 hours using an 11-point NRS ranging from 0 ("No urgency") to 10 ("Worst possible urgency").
L748: Abdominal pain is another common symptom of UC that is an important treatment objective for patients. The Applicant assessed abdominal pain with the Abdominal Pain NRS PRO measure, a single item PRO measure assessing severity of a subject's abdominal pain within the past 24 hours using an 11-point NRS ranging from 0 ("No Pain") to 10 ("Pain as Bad as Can Imagine").
L749: The Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS were collected at baseline, Week 12, and Week 52. An ad hoc analysis of the proportion of subjects with "absence of urgency" defined as an Urgency NRS score of 0 and "absence of abdominal pain" defined as an Abdominal Pain NRS score of 0 was performed at Week 12 and Week 52. Of note, no data were collected between these timepoints.
L750: A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Urgency NRS of 0 than placebo at Week 12 (19.3% vs 7.4%) and Week 52 (18.6% vs 8.1%). A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Abdominal Pain NRS of 0 than placebo at Week 12 (26.6% vs 13.3%) and Week 52 (21.5% vs 7.4%).
L751: Although bowel urgency and abdominal pain are common and important symptoms of UC, the results of the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS are not recommended for inclusion in labeling due to the ad hoc nature of the analyses (i.e., not multiplicity controlled and performed after unblinding). Additionally, given the recall period of "the past 24 hours" for the bowel urgency and abdominal pain assessments, data collected at baseline, Week 12, and Week 52 would only be representative of patients' experiences in the past 24 hours at each respective assessment time point. Therefore, the results are difficult to interpret as UC is a chronic disease that can relapse and remit.
L752: ## Inflammatory Bowel Disease Questionnaire
L753: The inflammatory bowel disease questionnaire (IBDQ) is an instrument used to assess health-related quality of life. The Division had previously determined that IBDQ is not appropriate to support labeling claims. The instrument combines many concepts and impacts, and some of the questions are double-barreled or otherwise unclear. Therefore, the results of the IBDQ are not recommended for inclusion in labeling.
L754: ## Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
L755: Efficacy results were investigated by prior biologic or JAK inhibitor exposure status for multiplicity-controlled endpoints in APD334-301, as summarized in Table 34. Although the trial was not powered to detect a significant difference for each subgroup, the treatment differences were consistent with the primary analysis. The etrasimod group consistently showed higher remission and response rates compared to the placebo group across all subgroups. In general, the observed remission and response rates were higher in the subgroup with no prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure versus the subgroup with prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure for both etrasimod and placebo, which was expected because patients with prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure are typically more refractory to treatment. The subgroup with no prior exposure to biologics/JAK inhibitors generally had a larger treatment difference between etrasimod and placebo, though the estimated treatment differences for endoscopic improvement and mucosal healing endpoints were similar across subgroups.

L769: The subgroup analyses for efficacy conducted by use of corticosteroids at baseline for the co-primary endpoints were presented in Table 35. L775: ## 8.1.3. APD334-302
L776: ## Trial Design
L777: Trial APD334-302 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg, compared with placebo, over a 12-week induction period. The trial population included subjects with a modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 4 to 9 who had an inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1 of the following: oral 5-aminosalicylic acid (5-ASA) compounds, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologic therapy (TNF blocker, vedolizumab, anti-IL 12/23), or Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors. A total of 354 subjects, including 2 adolescent subjects, from 39 countries were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 12 weeks. Randomization was stratified by prior treatment status (i.e., naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry [yes/no]), baseline corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]). The overall trial design for APD334-302 is shown in Figure 11.
L778: Figure 11. Trial Design for APD334-302
L779: Source: clinical study report for APD334-302, Figure 1 (page 20).
L780: Abbreviation: JAK, Janus kinase; UC, ulcerative colitis
L781: ## Trial Endpoints
L782: The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12. The Applicant's definition was the same as APD334-301; refer to Section 8.1.1 for additional details. The multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoints are shown in Table 36 and were subject to overall type I error control (tested based on a prespecified gatekeeping approach). Refer to Section 16.4.1 for additional details on the multiple testing procedure.
L783: Table 36. Multiplicity-Controlled Secondary Efficacy Endpoints for Study APD334-302
L784: Secondary Endpoint	Definition
Endoscopic improvement at Week 12	ES \le 1 (excluding friability)
Symptomatic remission at Week 12	SFS = 0 (or = 1 with a \ge 1 point decrease from baseline) and RBS of 0
Mucosal healing at Week 12	ES of \le 1 (excluding friability) with histologic remission measured by a Geboes Index score < 2.0
L785: Source: Review team's table
L786: Abbreviation: ES, endoscopic subscore; SFS, stool frequency subscore; RBS, rectal bleeding subscore
L787: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L788: ## Populations
L789: The Applicant's primary efficacy analyses were based on subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9. The FAS consisted of all randomized subjects who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment.
L790: The two adolescent subjects were excluded from the efficacy analysis population, resulting in the "FDA Preferred Analysis Population." The safety population included all randomized subjects (including adolescents and subjects with mMS of 4) who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment, which is used for safety-related analysis.
L791: ## Sample Size
L792: Based on a 2-sided Fisher's exact test with a 1-sided significance level of 0.025, and a 2:1 randomization ratio, a sample size of 330 subjects (220 etrasimod, 110 placebo) was expected to provide at least 90% power to detect a difference of 12.5% in the primary endpoint of clinical remission between the etrasimod treatment group and the placebo treatment group, assuming a 6.0% remission rate for the placebo group.
L793: ## Calculation of SFS and RBS
L794: Similar to APD334-301, the primary efficacy endpoint for APD334-302 was evaluated using the mMS, which contains three components: SFS, RBS, and ES. For the SFS and RBS, the average of the most recent 3 consecutive days within the 7 days prior to the day of bowel preparation (excluding the day of bowel preparation) was rounded to the nearest integer to calculate the weekly score for each subject. If 3 consecutive days were not available, then the average of the 2 most recent consecutive days within the 7-day period was used. Sensitivity analyses were conducted that considered subjects with only two consecutive days as non-responders. The outcomes of these analyses were consistent with the primary efficacy results.
L795: ## Analyses of Efficacy Endpoints
L796: For assessments of the primary endpoint and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, the estimated difference in proportions between arms and the corresponding 95% confidence interval were computed based on the stratified risk difference using the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) weights. The analyses adjusted for the following randomization stratification factors: naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (yes/no), baseline corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]).
L797: ## Estimands and Intercurrent Events
L798: Similar to APD334-301, the estimand corresponding to the primary efficacy endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12 evaluated the difference in the proportion of subjects in clinical remission at Week 12 for etrasimod 2 mg versus placebo in subjects with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 in the FAS population. For the intercurrent events of 1) premature treatment discontinuation for worsening of disease, lack of efficacy, or adverse event related to UC, 2) initiation of a rescue medication for UC, 3) an increase in dose over baseline levels in subjects' existing UC medication, or 4) rescue medical procedure during the trial, subjects were considered non-responders.
L799: ## Handling of Missing Data
L800: For analyses of the efficacy endpoint and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, missing data were imputed using non-responder imputation (NRI). Sensitivity analyses for all multiplicity-controlled endpoints included multiple imputation under missing at random (MAR), tipping point analysis, multiple imputation with Copy Reference (CR) under missing not at
L801: random (MNAR), and a hybrid imputation with multiple imputation used to handle missing endoscopy data due to the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic and NRI used for missing data not due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
L802: ## Multiplicity Adjustment
L803: A gatekeeping multiple testing procedure was specified to control the overall Type I error rate at 2-sided alpha of 0.05, for all co-primary endpoints and major secondary endpoints. Refer to Figure 30 in Section 16.4.1 for the details of the gatekeeping procedure.
L804: ## **Protocol Amendments**
L805: The original study protocol for APD334-302 had three amendments. A summary of changes for each amendment are described in Table 37.
L806: **Table 37. Summary of Changes for APD334-302 Protocol Amendments**
L807: Amendment	Date	Summary of Changes
1.0	March 5, 2019	Additional monitoring procedures were added to the cardiac monitoring required on Day 1 after the first dose of study treatment. Post-dose safety vital signs, 12-lead ECGs, monitoring of subjects who do not meet the discharge criteria, and discontinuation rules were revised to align with FDA feedback.

2.0	February 7, 2020	Eligibility criteria related to contraceptive use was updated.
Additional instructions for safety monitoring related to ophthalmoscopy and optical coherence tomography (OCT) testing were included.
The prohibition of concomitant use of QT prolonging drugs was removed based on the results of the QT study (Study APD334-008).
An additional safety follow-up visit was added at 4 weeks.
Clarification and further instructions for tuberculosis testing were added and the tuberculosis screening questionnaire was updated.

3.0	February 22, 2021	Guidance for conducting virtual and offsite visits was updated in the protocol.
Anti-arrhythmic drugs were added to prohibited concomitant therapies.
Language was updated regarding timing of screening OCTs and pulmonary function tests (PFTs).
An exclusion criterion was added regarding treatment with topical rectal Chinese medicine, enemas, or suppositories prior to randomization.
L808: None of the changes had a major impact on the overall study design, efficacy assessments, or negatively impacted collection of safety data.
L809: ## 8.1.4. Trial Results (APD334-302)
L810: ## Patient Disposition
L811: The “FDA Preferred Analysis Population” consisted of a total of 333 subjects, 221 in the etrasimod 2 mg group and 112 in the placebo group, 89% (N=197) of whom completed the trial in the etrasimod group and 88% (N=99) in the placebo group. The disposition of subjects in the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population” was similar to the FAS.
L812: The most frequently reported reason for trial discontinuation in the etrasimod group was due to an adverse event. In the placebo group, the most frequently reported reason for trial discontinuation was withdrawal by subject. Table 38 summarizes subject disposition by treatment group.

L819: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L820: A total of 71 (21%) subjects had at least 1 important protocol deviation, including 50 (23%) subjects in the etrasimod group and 21 (19%) subjects in the placebo group. The most frequently reported (&gt; 5% subjects) important protocol deviations by category were “Study Procedures” (e.g., tests or assessments were not performed during the trial), “Laboratory Assessment”, “Eligibility and Entry Criteria” (e.g., subjects were randomized into the trial despite meeting exclusion criteria due to an oversight.), and “Visit Schedule.” The most common protocol deviations in both cohorts were “Study Procedures.” The number and distribution of protocol deviations were similar across both groups.
L821: ## Table of Demographic Characteristics
L822: Baseline demographic characteristics from the FDA preferred analysis population are presented in Table 39. Overall, the baseline demographics were similar to APD334-301. The majority of patients were male (57% and 63% in etrasimod and placebo, respectively) and most were
L823: White (75% and 78% in etrasimod and placebo groups, respectively). The mean age at consent was approximately 41 years for etrasimod and placebo groups, with a range in ages from 18-73 years and 18-72 years, respectively. The mean BMI was 24kg/m2 and 25 kg/m2 in etrasimod and placebo groups, respectively, ranging from 16kg/m2 to 40kg/m2 in the etrasimod group, and 15kg/m2 to 39kg/m2 in the placebo group. Demographic characteristics were generally similar among treatment groups.
L824: Similar to APD334-301, the majority of subjects were enrolled outside the U.S. However, as there are no well-established differences in UC disease severity or response to treatment by different ethnic groups, and the disease activity scores and documentation of prior treatment failure were standardized by the protocol, it is reasonable to generalize the result to the U.S. population.

L838: ## **Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use**
L839: Overall compliance was calculated as the total number of tablets taken divided by the total number of tablets expected during the treatment period. Subjects who took less than 80% or more than 120% of investigational drug during the entire treatment period were considered non-compliant. Treatment compliance was 99.6% for the etrasimod and 98.3% for the placebo group, respectively.
L840: Subjects must have had inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1 prior approved therapy for UC, including oral ASAs, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologics, and JAK inhibitors. Oral 5-ASA, AZA, 6-MP, oral corticosteroids, or medicinal probiotics were permitted at screening and as per the inclusion criteria (Trial APD334-302 Protocol Section 4.1). Concomitant treatments for UC were required to be stable prior to and during the trial and could not be initiated during screening or the treatment periods. Immunosuppressive agents such as oral AZA and 6-MP were to be discontinued \ge 2 weeks prior to randomization. The majority of subjects received at least 1 concomitant medication for UC during the trial (93% in the etrasimod group and 89% in the placebo group), the majority of which were oral 5-ASAs.
L841: ## **Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint**
L842: As discussed in Section 8.1.2, unless otherwise specified, all efficacy results in this review are based on the FDA Preferred Analysis Population defined as all adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9. The FDA preferred definition of clinical remission was used in the analyses of clinical remission related endpoints.
L843: The Applicant's efficacy results were confirmed by the review team. As shown in Table 41, the etrasimod treatment group had a statistically higher proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12 (26.2%) in comparison with those treated with placebo (15.2%), p-value = 0.010.
L844: **Table 41. Results for the Primary Endpoint, FDA Preferred Analysis Population, APD334-302**

L848: 2 The 2-sided p-value is to test the hypothesis of the treatment difference being 0.
L849: Abbreviations: N, number of subjects in treatment group; QD, once daily; n, number of events; CI, confidence interval
L850: Of note, the treatment difference observed for APD334-302 for clinical remission at Week 12 was smaller than the treatment difference observed for APD334-301 for clinical remission at Week 12. Specifically, the proportion of patients in clinical remission at Week 12 was higher in
L851: the placebo group (15.2%) in APD334-302 versus the placebo group (7.5%) in APD334-301. However, baseline demographic and disease characteristics were similar between APD334-301 and APD334-302. In response to an information request by the review team, the Applicant analyzed potentially contributing factors, but did not identify any covariates that fully explained the difference observed between the clinical remission rates of the two trials. The difference in placebo response was thus attributed to the intrinsic variability of the placebo response rate, which has been described to range from 5% to 25% in the literature (Jairath et al. 2017).
L852: Twenty-two subjects (10%) in the etrasimod group and 11 subjects (9.8%) in the placebo group had missing values. Most missing data was due to trial dropout with main reason due to adverse events for the etrasimod group and withdrawal by subject for the placebo group. Similar to APD334-301, non-responder imputation was applied to handle missing data. The results from the pre-specified missing data sensitivity analyses (described in Section 8.1.3) supported the conclusions of the primary analysis.

L871: ## Discussion of Primary and Secondary Endpoints Proposed for Labeling
L872: ## Symptomatic Remission
L873: As previously discussed in this document, symptomatic remission is not considered appropriate for inclusion in labeling because symptomatic remission is redundant to clinical remission. However, as discussed in Section 8.1.2 for APD334-301, information related to improvements in SFS, and RBS is useful to clinicians to help characterize the anticipated onset of improvement in symptoms. Refer to the Discussion of Additional Endpoints below.
L874: The consideration for other multiplicity-controlled endpoints (i.e., endoscopic improvement and histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement) is similar to those described for APD334-301. Refer to Section 8.1.2.
L875: ## Discussion of Additional Endpoints
L876: ## Stool Frequency and Rectal Bleeding Subscores
L877: Individual analyses of the SFS and RBS of the mMS in APD334-302 provided evidence that subjects treated with etrasimod reported symptomatic relief within a few weeks of treatment (Figure 12 and Figure 13). In comparison to the placebo group, subjects in the etrasimod treatment group exhibited a nominally significantly greater mean reduction in SFS (nominal p-value = 0.0128) and RBS (nominal p-value = 0.0107) as early as Week 2 based on estimates derived from an MMRM.
L878: **Figure 12. Stool Frequency Subscore Change from Baseline, FDA Preferred Analysis Population, APD334-302**
L879: Source: Reviewer generated figure based on the Applicant submitted data adqs.xpt.
L880: Estimates are from a mixed-effect model with repeated measures for change from baseline with a covariate for baseline score, and factors for naive to biologic/JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (Yes/No), baseline corticosteroid use (Yes/No), baseline disease activity (mMS: 4 to 6 or 7 to 9), treatment, visit, treatment by visit interaction.
L881: Abbreviations: LSM, least-square mean; CI, confidence interval.
L882: **Figure 13. Rectal Bleeding Subscore Change from Baseline, FDA Preferred Analysis Population, APD334-302**
L883: Source: Reviewer generated figure based on the Applicant submitted data adqs.xpt.
L884: Estimates are from a mixed-effect model with repeated measures for change from baseline with a covariate for baseline score, and factors for naive to biologic/JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (Yes/No), baseline corticosteroid use (Yes/No), baseline disease activity (mMS: 4 to 6 or 7 to 9), treatment, visit, treatment by visit interaction.
L885: Abbreviations: LSM, least-square mean; CI, confidence interval.
L886: For APD334-302, there was a trend toward more subjects experiencing improvement in SFS and RBS at Week 2. Given that it was not until Week 4 that a significant difference between etrasimod and placebo for the RBS was observed in APD334-301, the review team recommended aligning the timepoint between APD334-301 and APD334-302 for the RBS due to the importance of replication to ensure observed results are not due to change. Therefore, decreases RBS being observed as early as Week 4 will be descriptively included in labeling.
L887: The considerations for other endpoints that are not controlled for multiplicity (i.e., clinical response, endoscopic remission, and endoscopic remission and Geboes histologic score &lt;2.0) are similar to those outlined for APD334-301. Refer to Section 8.1.2 for discussion regarding the endpoints proposed for labeling.
L888: ## **Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints**
L889: Refer to Section 8.1.2. for discussion regarding the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS. Similar to APD334-301, the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS were collected at baseline and Week 12. An ad hoc analysis of the proportion of subjects with “absence of urgency” defined as an Urgency NRS score of 0 and “absence of abdominal pain” defined as an
L890: Abdominal Pain NRS score of 0 was performed at Week 12. Of note, no data were collected between these timepoints.
L891: A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Urgency NRS of 0 than placebo at Week 12 (21.2% vs 11.6%). A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Abdominal Pain NRS of 0 than placebo at Week 12 (31.5% vs 17.9%).
L892: Similar to APD334-301, although bowel urgency and abdominal pain are common and important symptoms of UC, the results of the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS are not recommended for inclusion in labeling due to the ad hoc nature of the analyses (i.e., not multiplicity controlled and performed after unblinding). Refer to Section 8.1.2 for further discussion of these endpoints.
L893: ## **Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial**
L894: The efficacy results for multiplicity-controlled endpoints in APD334-302 were analyzed based on prior biologic or JAK inhibitor exposure status, as presented in Table 44. While the trial was not designed to detect a significant difference for each subgroup, the treatment differences observed were in line with the primary analysis. Across all subgroups, the etrasimod groups consistently demonstrated numerically higher rates of remission and response compared to the placebo. The subgroup of subjects without prior exposure to biologic or JAK inhibitors exhibited numerically higher remission and response rates than those with prior exposure.

L907: ## 8.1.5. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L908: ## Subpopulations
L909: Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect at Week 12 and at Week 52 for various demographic characteristics. Overall, the etrasimod group consistently showed higher clinical remission rates compared to placebo across all subgroups, as detailed in Table 46, Table 47, and Table 48. In APD334-301, female subjects within the etrasimod group had a clinical remission rate approximately 17% higher at Week 12 compared to the male subjects; however, the difference was not evident in APD334-302. In the placebo group of APD334-302, female subjects had around 10% clinical remission rate higher at Week 12 compared to male subjects; however, the discrepancy was not evident in APD334-301. Such variations might be attributed to the constrained sample sizes of the subgroups.

L925: ## 8.1.6. Conclusions on Effectiveness
L926: Overall, the submission contains substantial evidence of effectiveness for etrasimod 2 mg daily based on data from two adequate and well-controlled investigations. The results of the 52-week trial (APD334-301) were statistically and clinically significant compared to placebo on the primary and multiple major secondary endpoints. Similarly, the results for the 12-week trial (APD334-302) were statistically and clinically significant compared to placebo on the primary
L927: and multiple major secondary endpoints. The results of both trials were robust to subgroup analyses, including for subjects with prior biologic or JAKi failure, baseline corticosteroid use, and mMS greater than 5, supporting that clinical benefit was observed among the trial population. The submitted evidence supports the conclusion that etrasimod is effective in the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adult patients.
L928: ## 8.2. Review of Safety
L929: ## 8.2.1. Safety Review Approach
L930: The safety evaluation of etrasimod for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC focused on data from the randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003, and is supplemented by additional data from an integrated summary of safety (ISS). The ISS was a pool of controlled data from studies in subjects with UC including APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003 as well as uncontrolled data from the open-label extension studies APD334-303, APD334-005, and ES101002.
L931: APD334-301 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 52 weeks. After Week 12, subjects who met the prespecified definition of UC worsening were eligible to enter the open-label extension study (APD334-303).
L932: Safety data from APD334-302 and APD334-003 were pooled ("12-Week Safety Pool") because the two study designs and populations were sufficiently similar, to better assess the adverse event profile for the 12-week trials. APD334-302 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 12 weeks. APD334-003 was a phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg and 1 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily, etrasimod 1 mg once daily, or matching placebo for up to 12 weeks. The 1 mg etrasimod arm of APD334-003 was not pooled because this dose was not evaluated in APD334-301 or APD334-302. Due to the differences in randomization ratios, pooled analysis results were adjusted for the study size.
L933: The ISS consisted of controlled and uncontrolled data from studies in subjects with UC, as described previously. Since much of the data in the ISS was uncontrolled, the ISS was not considered to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness or safety. However, safety data were reviewed to assess for rare events given the extended duration of treatment for the open-label studies.
L934: Clinical trial data were analyzed using JMP and JMP Clinical software. All safety assessments and conclusions are those of the clinical review team unless otherwise specified. The clinical review team recoded several adverse event terms using logical groupings in order to better ascertain the safety profile of etrasimod; refer to Table 107 in Section 16.5 of the Appendix. The clinical review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review.
L935: ## 8.2.2. Review of the Safety Database
L936: ## Overall Exposure
L937: The overall safety database that was the focus of the safety review consisted of 891 subjects as described in Table 49. A total of 577 subjects received etrasimod 2 mg. A total of 3 adolescent subjects were enrolled in the overall safety database. Data from these adolescent subjects were included in the safety analyses; however, due to the limited data, the efficacy analyses were focused on adult subjects, and it was not recommended to include adolescents in the proposed indication.
